The Weir, the lowland road.** The stand has been dead for about twenty-six hours and the plate has printed nothing, because a plate with no term in force is not a fault. **Jonas tries to read it and there is nothing to read, and that is the volume's first refusal and it is a correct one.** Perdita Sow is up the path with a shopping bag and a sling on her right hand — out of Saint Orra east since the Friday, three nights, given eleven days — and she tells him what five weeks of writing on walls has looked like from a road: *on Monday I could not tell you which of the two of us you are.* **Margo Doré says the stand answers to the mains, and a supply is not a service, and that she burnt a water-authority letter in March about her own supply and will not say what was in it, and makes him write that down in full and read it back.** They clear nine more inches of trap by hand, three people, unpaid, not a repair and not on anything. Margo Doré tells him to say a sentence in a fortnight in a room where it matters.

### Chapter 0050 — Six Days Is Not a Term Any More

**Monday 21 April 22:00 into Tuesday 22 April morning. Saint Orra west, then Halvorsen's office.** The 22:00 handover makes a **thirty-two-hour** term — the fourth short term in nine days, because *no six-day term until the rota is built again*, written in the handover book in Ruth Nwosu's hand. The plate prints `THIS NODE IS NOT ON A CONTINUITY SCHEDULE. A NODE NOT ON A SCHEDULE IS NOT DISCONNECTED. IT IS DEFERRED.` **CivicCore's continuity assessment arrives: four nodes on the west site chain are in failure under paragraph 11 — 6-44 mortuary van gate, staff stair 2, car park barrier 3, service riser 1. Tiers are £1,880 / £4,200 / £11,400. Halvorsen has £4,200 and must write to one person by Saturday.** He asks Jonas which node is worth £4,200 a year. **Jonas refuses and tells him to write to the mortuary porter and find out that he was right.** Then he opens 6-44 and finds that the gate is mechanically perfect and the fault is a **sixth field with a cursor in it and nothing in it: LAST VERIFIED ESSENTIAL USE.** He writes four lines in a pad and telephones the registrar's office, which says the registrar will look at it on the Friday.

### Chapter 0051 — The Field Nobody Fills In

**Tuesday 22 April into Wednesday 23 April, 07:20.** Four names gone through and nothing written. The facilities manager's deputy will not sign a sentence he cannot answer for in June. The trust board does not resolve before nine on a Friday. **The mortuary porter refuses in four seconds: *I have not got the people who do not come in a van.*** **Bea Osunde, at the bench: *you do not get to be modest on behalf of a mortuary gate. That is not restraint, that is a vacuum with your face in it*** — and then, unprompted, that not having a plan was the correct thing to do. **Jonas has it on a pad on the Tuesday and does not go to the yard.** At 07:20 on Wednesday 6-44 prints `A DEFERRED NODE SERVES ITS LAST VERIFIED ESSENTIAL USE. IF NONE IS RECORDED, THE USE IS THE NODE'S MAINTENANCE.` He puts it in the public box, and Mina Okafor asks him to tell her she is wrong. He goes to a panel he was not invited to, and a CivicCore continuity field manager is in it.

### Chapter 0052 — The Appeal That Everybody Expected to Fail

**Wednesday 23 April, 09:30 to 13:10. The Cooper's Reach panel.** **The order is upheld**, on a subsubstitution: the premises' water and refuse services are recorded as **a supply and not a service**, and no civic claim attaches to a supply. Prue Calloway uses section nine of the panel rules for the first time in eleven years and asks for it to be minuted. Ferdy Oduya cannot get at the refuge store because the key is on a seven-to-seven clock. **Jonas does not ask for the cliff-side lorry even though it will be there tomorrow at ten past eight, and says so out loud instead.** **Nell Ardent reads clause 4(c) out of a document she has carried since 2 April: a signature is a consent mark and may not be withdrawn except on the same date next year.** And she refuses to make it easier: nobody was deceived, and she has spent eleven years getting people to sign things and has never once been able to un-sign one. **Yasmin Rohan refuses CivicCore for the third time, in a third room, and this time the sentence is longer and gives a reason she has not given before, which is that in week twelve the people who signed nothing find out the thing they were using was bought, and by then they have a habit.** The chair's worse, helpful finding: the only route that would have won it is a location of record, and a panel cannot weigh a claim from a person it cannot locate. **Her participation figures are the association's, not Wren Court's: a hundred and nineteen adults read a page of numbers in a room in daylight and signed, and nine of the hundred and twenty-eight have missed a month.** Jonas puts the green pillar and the burnt letter side by side on a stairwell and then stops, because a post on a street with no address is a post and a woman of sixty-three who burns her own letter is a person, and a schedule is about the first and a panel's finding is about the second, and a committee in nine days is going to use a document about the first to answer a question about the second.

### Chapter 0053 — Nine Seconds on a Delivery Note

**Wednesday 23 April 23:40 into Thursday 24 April, 07:20.** A mortuary van comes through 6-44 and Ivo Bamba, 29, on since ten, on nobody's rota, stands in the yard afterwards and writes in about nine seconds, in biro, on the back of a laundry delivery note, with no date and no initials: `ANYBODY WITH A DEAD BODY OR A PERSON WHO CANNOT GET IN ON THEIR OWN`. **Halvorsen rings at 06:40. Jonas goes to the yard and tells him exactly what he has done, that it cannot be withdrawn, and that he must not be asked to take it back — and does not ask.** Ivo Bamba's reason: *I did not do it for the hospital. Forty minutes is not about a door. Forty minutes is about a family.* He says *then it stays* and goes to bed for eleven hours. **Jonas writes line four of a pad: *I did not go to the yard on Tuesday. I had it on a pad on Tuesday.*** **Oren Pell refuses to strike it in about ninety seconds** — a hollow term is a permission that functions; striking it empties the field; an empty field means the node's maintenance; the maintenance on a mortuary gate is a man with a key — and then says he has struck four in nineteen years, that there are about nine hundred, and that **this is a defect of the design and not of maintenance.**

### Chapter 0054 — The Meeting in the Community Room

**Thursday 24 April, 20:00 to 23:00. Wren Court, Salter's Yard.** She told the thirty-one first. Her three conditions, the third being that **the fee does not stop**. The file: **nineteen of thirty-one signed on 12 March, four have missed a month, the fee is £11 to £13.60 a week**, and the answer to any request for a list of people is the address of Wren Court. **Yolanda Fofana's own signature lapsed on 1 April, the block went thin for eleven days, and she told the block meeting on the 8th that there was *a mains matter*.** And she gives him the shape of it, which she has not said out loud to anybody including the eleven people who were in that building on the 18th: **on the night of the seventeenth eleven people were in that building and all eleven were on a lapsed term and the water was at half.** Jonas tells her about the mortuary gate and reads his own fourth pad line out. She says: the two are the same shape and they are not the same size. The meter cupboard carries `THE HOLDER OF THIS POINT PAYS FOR IT. THE POINT IS NOT THE PAYER'S.` She predicts he will come back on Sunday and tells him to come and not to pretend it was for the file.

### Chapter 0055 — The Second Named Clinician

**Friday 25 April, 09:40. The corridor at Saint Orra west.** She sent a message; he brought the wrong thing. **She has a second named clinician — Priya Ranganathan, 41, from 2 May — and it exists because Gunnar Halvorsen found about nine hundred pounds in a dead 2011 participation line item and wrote an order under the site administration on 22 April.** The fix and the disease are the same money, and she says so before he can, and asks him to be careful that nobody puts the two in the same paragraph in June. **She gives him the four refusals' reasons** — a person instead of a rota line; I will not be on a rota I did not staff; **the education office, whose whole letter was *the post is not vacant***; and *not from you* — for work and not for trust, and says she has got four of them and no one to hand them to. **She will not let him thank her.** And she warns him that with two clinicians on the roster the argument in his own head is going to sound like duty, and it is not duty, it is the third of April with a rota and a hole in it. **Do not come into a room in June with something I have said in a corridor in April in your pocket.** He puts the pad on the sill and lets her read the mortuary gate.

### Chapter 0056 — Do Not Come

**Sunday 27 April. The shop yard, then the Saint Orra bridge.** A contractor's covering submission quotes **Tomas Renn's own finding 1** in support of a procurement remedy — *continuity should be held by a party able to guarantee it, and the guarantee should be priced* — and cites the minority report about his two dissenting stewards in a footnote. **He knew the cost on the Friday and paid it anyway, and says the document is a good argument.** **He asks Jonas not to come to 19 May**, and gives the reason: in that room the sentence stops being a shop steward's and becomes a thing the man in the register said, and he wrote it down so it would survive him being wrong. **Jonas agrees. It is the first time he has chosen not to use the register.** Oren Pell tells him he may not attend the 30 April pre-sitting and why. **Dev Singh has had the empty-crossing clause on a file for nine days with no paid hours on it, and has worked out why he never asked Jonas for it**, and asks for it to be written on a card in the coat and not in the shop. He puts two numbers together: **nineteen days next to forty minutes.**

### Chapter 0057 — Nobody Has Asked Her To

**Monday 28 April, 10:00 to 11:30. The Carrow Lane Housing Association, two rooms above a betting shop.** **Marguerite Oyelaran, 47, eleven years, no assistant.** Four blocks, 128 rooms, Tier 2 bought on 11 March for £16,800, passed on at £2.60 a room a week by putting a standing order form through 128 letterboxes herself on Saturdays. The reason is the lift in Block D and a man of eighty-three on four and a board that said no in ninety seconds. **Her own counts, and they are hers: one hundred and twenty of a hundred and twenty-eight signed on the sheet she hands over, with a second sheet behind it carrying two more she has been back to since Friday — one hundred and twenty-two total, and six who have not signed, five of the eight refusals saying *I will not sign a thing that makes my name a door*, and eleven of the hundred and twenty-two in Block C on a lapsed term on 18 April with the water at half.** She says the page has block and room numbers and no names, that the answer to any request for a list of the people in her stock is two rooms above a betting shop, and that rule 4(c) is worth three hundred and seventy-five days and is worse than nothing after that. **Her line: *I did not invent the price. I passed it on because I had nowhere to put it.*** **Jonas's first transfer attempt fails, on Volume 01's own rule:** he asks her to put her name in six lowland holder fields; she says yes in four seconds; then declines, because a plate will not take a bare role and an officer findable at six water points on a street with no address is a document with a name on it. **She says yes in four seconds to a thing she knows is wrong, out loud, and asks him to write it down.** The meter room carries `A TIER MAY BE CHALLENGED BY A DISTRICT OR BY A NODE IN THE SAME TIER. IT MAY NOT BE CHALLENGED BY A PERSON.` And she tells him the number: **six lowland points at Tier 1 is £11,280 a year, and she worked it out on 12 March, and nobody has asked her.** He does not ask. He writes it in her words and she puts it on her own board in her own hand, at 10 past eleven, having said she would be able to look at him afterwards.

### Chapter 0058 — The Main Is Sound

**Tuesday 29 April 23:40 into Wednesday 30 April, 07:40.** A commissioning clamp, fitted 11 April with a removal date of 18 April, still on a lowland spur joint, and a works order closed on paper on the twentieth by a crew that never came back. **Emeka Rusk finds it from a gauge and they take it off in fifty minutes at ten past one in the morning and the noise goes up the lowland for a mile.** The main is sound and that is not the point: **the six points are not a pipe, they are a term, the term is a price, and there is nobody on the tier at one o'clock in the morning.** The duty engineer says no on the telephone in the plainest professional English Jonas has heard in five weeks. **Wendell Pryce, CivicCore continuity field manager, has been in a car on the embankment since half past twelve because a gauge they maintain dropped, and offers to put the lowland on Tier 2 in eleven minutes for £25,200, and says three things: it works; it is a purchase and not a favour, and he cannot make it a favour without creating a rota of nights; and he was told the third reason and it is true.** Then he adds the fourth one himself, about a tender in two years for *emergency discretionary continuity, section four*, priced and awarded on a track record of discretionary actions. **They do not buy it.** Six hours and ten minutes with no mains water on the lowland, from twenty to twelve on the Tuesday to ten to six on the Wednesday, when what came back was a written declaration and a trickle; six bottles carried up a hundred and nine steps at ten to five in the morning by Aurrie Nkemdirim, sixty-one, not on any rota, for Marigold Adjei, seventy-seven, with a chest; **and nobody wrote it down, and if anybody had, she would have asked them to take it out.** Margo Doré makes him say the sentence in her kitchen and then makes him promise not to come up the path at two in the morning. **And the Weir trap is lost for good: no term, no recorded use, therefore the use is the node's maintenance, and the maintenance is the pump that is in the trap.** The notice at the standpipe: `THE ORDER OF TIERS IS SET BY WHAT IS PAID. IT IS NOT SET BY WHAT IS NEEDED.` He writes down the thing the volume is: the thing standing between a street and its water may be a thing nobody has ever said no to, because nobody has ever needed to, because it has never been in a room.

### Chapter 0059 — The Return of a Deferred Node

**Wednesday 30 April, 10:40 to 20:40, closing on a coda dated Tuesday 20 May. The Old Ward porch, then a stairwell.** Jonas is not in the pre-sitting and is told he may not be. **Nina Calder has put Schedule 4 into three systems, and the chair has admitted four non-members to a closed meeting, and Alden Rook will be in it.** Nobody asked for the form. **The Lattice cannot publish a narrowing — the drawing layer draws a shape, a term, a date and a claim, and has never once drawn what a thing stopped doing — and the city can.** The **Return of a Deferred Node** is printed at half past ten that morning by a records clerk who has never printed one, and issued that evening to nine parties: ten fields, nine lines, **a blank rule at the bottom that is not a design feature.** The first return, read aloud in the porch in the rain at six o'clock, in order, one to nine: seven forms back out of nine asked, six of them carrying nine nodes, one signed *unknown* and blank, two silent and published as two. **Eight nodes with a holder, one without** — a lowland water point, no number, no location of record, no district, no tier, serving *a pump*** — and the answer to *what would be needed to restore* is the same on every line: **a person, a price, or a schedule.** Pell rules out naming it in about forty seconds: a location of record would be a survey, a survey is a count, a count is the price. **Rook, in a stairwell, twenty minutes, honestly:** the challenge clause is the best clause in his document and is also the reason a man on an embankment can be beaten by an invoice; he is not going to define *holder* and says so unasked; he will publish CivicCore's own narrowings on her form in a fortnight because the first thing that happened to that lowland at one in the morning was that a contractor's pressure system noticed, not a person. And: **in about two years somebody will use the register to say the city has an administrator.** On Tuesday 20 May the fourth return carries a Cooper's Reach clinic with forty-one patients, nine of them over seventy-five and three housebound, and the answer to *who answers for it* is **nobody**.

### Chapter 0060 — The Number in a Chair

**Thursday 1 May into Friday 2 May. The Old Ward porch, then the shop, then a telephone.** The porch has **nine plastic chairs, numbered one to nine in chalky white paint by a caretaker so that people who come every day can find the same chair**, and four is worn through. That is the batch's whole argument in an object: *you number a chair; a chair is not a household.* **A form that is not adopted, not minuted and not enforceable, on which no party is required to answer, is not a register — it is a piece of paper with a crest on it and a hole in the bottom** (Bea Osunde, at the bench, with a plate). Kofi Sarr has been asked by the council flood forum a second time and said no again and the man wrote *declined*; **Nora Bell has now been asked three times for a seat, an allowance and a badge, and on the third asking she did not answer at all and walked off down the middle of the crossing, which Bea says is worse.**

**The major turn: the second return carries Ibrahim Sesay's school gate with the word A KEY in the holder field**, because a records clerk put a man in a field on the first pass and was stopped, and the field would not take blank, and there was nowhere else to put it. Doina Serkis apologised to him by telephone. **His answer: *the line is wrong and the line is mine, and the key is the honest answer and the key is wrong, and I am not going to go round this borough saying the city has said my name is a gate.*** **A holder field on a form is not a holder. It is a box.** Oren Pell gives ninety seconds and will not say what Rook said in the pre-sitting — *I was in that room for forty minutes and there is no minute of it and nobody in this city can tell you what was said afterwards, including the man who said it* — and warns that the department's laminated-card nineteen-day median must not be put in a member's mouth as a city figure.

**Ending: Halvorsen telephones at about seven.** He wrote to the mortuary porter again; the reply is on hospital writing paper with a date and a signature and reads *I still have not got the people who do not come in a van*, and Halvorsen will not read the signature out because he will not hand Jonas a name on a telephone. **The gate has been open every night since 00:01 on 26 April because of Ivo Bamba's four words in biro. The other three nodes have been on nothing for fifteen days.** The decision is **Saturday 10 May, 10:00, four nodes, four documents, £4,200, and Jonas is not in the room and did not ask twice.**

### Chapter 0061 — Two Pounds Sixty

**Friday 2 May into Saturday 3 May. The shop yard, then the Carrow Lane Housing Association.** Jonas does the arithmetic in the yard and it is the chapter: **£2.60 a room a week is £135.20 a room a year; 122 paying rooms is £16,494.40, so the association is £305.60 short of the £16,800 it spent, and the cost of the six who would not sign is eight hundred and eleven pounds and twenty pence, to the penny — £505.60 of margin it never had plus the £305.60 hole, all of it out of the same three hundred and five pounds sixty — and nobody in that office has ever worked it out.** He gives it back to Nina Calder and asks only for the March consumption figures. *Go on. Friday afternoon. And do not go on Saturday, because Saturday morning she has a void.*

**Block C is a void.** Thirty rooms, 1938, shared stopcock in a cupboard nobody has a key to, eight households carrying the water in buckets since 1998, twenty-two households moved since Sunday out of a void allowance, and a letter of 29 April from an agent isolating the supply on **Tuesday 6 May**. **Block C going empty is £4,056 a year of income that has stopped, and the shortfall goes from £305.60 to £4,361.60, and the void allowance is nil in October.** She says this before he can.

**A woman of sixty from Block B room eleven comes up the stairs with a shift at two.** In writing she can leave the agreement in a fortnight; on the plate her consent mark cannot be withdrawn until 12 March next year. *From June you are not paying me and for ten months after the third of June you are still counted as having consented, and the Lattice grades you, and your grade decides how far down your own water goes if the money ever stops.* **Nell Ardent, at the window:** a mass withdrawal is a data set, a withdrawal rate is a number a landlord and a funder use, and in fourteen months somebody will say the block was a bad asset. **Marguerite Oyelaran:** *a data set is when somebody else makes the list. Those twelve people are making their own list, in their own handwriting, on their own doorsteps, on the same day, and I have never once seen it and I am not going to tell a woman of thirty-nine that it is a data set when it is the first honest thing on this road since I put a tier on four blocks in March.*

**The turn, and the first of the batch's four notices: the block's consumption is what the term is sized on, and it went up thirty-one per cent in April, because all four blocks were full for eight days after the surge and none of them was empty for the eleven days in March the price was calculated on. The fee is priced on a year in which these blocks are half empty and it is being paid for a year in which they are full, and the difference is in April's metered recharge on four invoices to a hundred and twenty-eight people and is not in a letter.** The plate prints: `A NODE WITH A HOLDER THAT HAS NOT BEEN RENEWED FOR ITS TERM SERVES THE TERM IT WAS LAST READ INTO. AND NOT THE TERM SOMEBODY EXPECTED.` He writes that he has two true numbers and one day of one of them, and has until Monday to work out how to say both or neither. **Twelve of the hundred and twenty-eight have asked to leave; eleven of them did it in one morning, in their own doorways, in the same hour, on a Tuesday.**

### Chapter 0062 — The Nineteenth of May Is Not a Hearing Yet

**Saturday 3 May into Sunday 4 May. The Old Ward, the first-floor corridor, and the records office.** **There are two pieces of paper describing Monday and neither author read the other.** The tabling list, item 5: *Schedule 4 (continuity pricing) — referred on.* The covering submission, paragraph 2: **the Committee is invited to APPROVE Schedule 4 and to ADOPT it as a schedule of the Committee for the remainder of the financial year.** Approving and adopting is not receiving; a member who reads only the list has a different picture from one who reads the submission, and nobody moves it, because a submission is a document and a tabling list is a document and a chair decides between them.

**Pell:** a registrar does not get to know what a committee is going to do before it does. **He may be in the gallery — a gallery is for people who want to listen and nobody in a gallery is a party — and if somebody puts a microphone on him Pell will stand up and say so.** That is the entire extent of what the register buys.

**Fenella Marsh refuses him the papers in a corridor, in one sentence, and it is not unkind** — not because he is not a party, which she thinks is a bad ground, but because in four minutes a man with a lanyard will ask whether the papers went to the man off the register and the truth is the better answer. **She will not rule on the discrepancy because that is not a chair's job; she says about four times in five it happens and does nothing, and about once in five somebody moves it and gets it, and by June the city has adopted a price list as a schedule of its own standing committee. She will know by Wednesday lunchtime whether there is a motion in the papers.**

**Nell Ardent is told by a member's office, in about four minutes, that only a member may table an amendment, that a member may move from the floor without notice, and that if four members had wanted it moved it would already be in the papers.** Nobody is hiding it. Four people have each independently judged that a definition of *holder* in a public sitting in May is worse than being criticised later for not moving it.

**The consequence, and it is a paperwork error: Nina Calder put Alden Rook's Wednesday-night stairwell prediction into the papers' background field at ten past five on Wednesday evening, in his words, with his name and a date — *a definition of the term holder would be drafted by whichever party places a schedule before the Committee* — because she would not state a true thing without saying whose it was.** The papers went to the clerk at nine on Friday and printed at two and again at eleven on Friday night, and it cannot come out, and on Monday it will be read out, and it is the truest thing anybody says all day, and it is in her field. *A sentence is not a thing that wins. It stops, for about a year, somebody being able to say the city does not know.*

**The clerk's cover note is one paragraph of four sentences and the fourth says the Return of a Deferred Node is not before the Committee, is not adopted, is not minuted, is not enforceable, establishes no service, no holder, no district and no number of nodes in this city, and is printed solely because the covering submission refers to the number of deferred nodes and **no other instrument in this city is capable of supplying that number.** Frances Tolley, committee clerk of twenty-two years, wrote it on Friday afternoon on purpose after reading the form and rang Nina to ask whether she minded. It will be read out first on Monday, slowly, and it cannot be got out.

### Chapter 0063 — The Saturday Decision

**Saturday 10 May, 10:00 onward. The yard at River Stacks until one, then the Saint Orra west corridor.** Jonas chooses not to be in the room and spends the morning doing ordinary work with a spanner, and Bea Osunde works it out from the spanner and does not say the sentence. *It is a quarter past twelve. Whatever it is that you are not doing, you are doing it with a spanner.*

**Nina Calder telephones with four facts she says she will tell him once.** One: **three of the four are not services.** A staff stair is not a service (*this is a stair*, Dermot Fahey, facilities supervisor, six years). A car park barrier is not a service (*car park barrier three is a car park barrier, it is not a service, there is nobody in this car park who is a customer, they are all staff, and I have been doing this job for eleven years and nobody has ever asked me what it is for and I have assumed it is a barrier* — Ines Tarrant). **And the riser is a pipe in a building** (Ronan Vasquez, estates, eleven years, who drew it in ninety seconds, crossed the two taps it serves, wrote it out in an enormous hand, and **took it off the list himself under his own holding in nine minutes, because the schedule lets the party holding the maintenance take a node off as well as put one on**). *Nobody in this city has ever asked whether a thing on a list of things to pay for is a thing.* Two: **the gate goes on it** — Tier 2, £4,200, 6-44, effective 11 May, **term to 06:00 on Sunday 12 October**. Three: **the care entrance plate printed at about twenty past twelve and it says six forty-one IS ON A CONTINUITY SCHEDULE. TIER 2**, and there is no field on that plate for a schedule and no field for a tier, because the care entrance was never a node in failure and the plate has five fields and a cursor and none of them is money. **The door of a hospital is inside a priced thing and the plate has nowhere to keep the price, and nobody has been billed and the trust's finance side says it will fall out of the year end.** Four: that is three and a half hours of Nina's Thursday and she is not on duty.

**Mina Okafor, at the plate, with about forty people going past:** *I have worked in this building for nine years and I have never in nine years seen a thing get put on a plate that the plate had not got a place for.* **Jonas cannot tell her it is wrong and says exactly why — he has not asked one question about the schedule in three weeks, because the schedule is a contractor's document and he has spent six weeks not asking anybody about one, and that has turned out to be a thing he cannot defend in a corridor.** She asks a third time what the care entrance costs; he answers: £4,200 for the node next door, the care entrance is on the same schedule at the same tier, the plate has nowhere to write a number, and he does not know. **She writes it in the box, in her own hand, with the date, under the forty minutes and not under the recent notices, because it is not a notice and it is an event** — *a woman asked on the tenth of May and nobody knew* — and asks for the date on it.

**Halvorsen in the corridor at twenty past four, unasked, in four minutes.** The mortuary porter came in at half past ten, did not know he had written the four words, and said: *that is mine, is it. Right. Then I would like it left where it is and I would like it not to be on a form* — put in the minute as **attended, declined to sign**. **Nothing was voted. The most consequential thing to happen to that site since the seventeenth of April was decided by one man with a folder and three other men and a porter who did not know he had written anything, and he could not have told you at eight in the morning which of the four he was going to do.** And then the question nobody asked: he has been reading *may place nodes on a schedule* since 22 April as a man with four nodes, and he has never once read it as a man who can put a *plate* on a schedule — and the plate is a **chain**, with two other things on it that are not nodes. **And the twelfth of October is a Sunday, which Ronan Vasquez said in the room at ten to one the way Jonas said the thing about the stair, and which nobody in the building has asked about, because every time Halvorsen asks one of those four people about a date, one of them ends up on a rota because of the asking. He will not ask. He says Jonas may tell them.** He says of Jonas: *I asked you on 21 April and got no in ninety seconds, and I have spent a fortnight about it.*

### Chapter 0064 — A Council Flood Forum

**Friday 2 May on the Saint Orra platform, then Tuesday 6 May in a room in the civic offices.** **Gareth Raynor-Hale asks Kofi Sarr the second question, which is not about a seat: *what happened on the night of the seventeenth on the lowland embankment*, from an operations review of 28 April in which somebody wrote *lowland evacuation capacity: unresolved*. Kofi, who has refused three times, gives an answer instead: bay four closed at 11 minutes past two, sixty-one on the fare machine, four and a half miles, about forty over seventy, nothing else, and asks Raynor-Hale to write it down. Afterwards: *you do not refuse a man for four months and then find out that the reason you refused him was not principle. It is that you did not have the number yet.** The Saint Orra and District Flood Forum is a council committee with eleven places, about ninety thousand pounds, **nine ordinary service districts, a hospital trust, a school, a market, and CivicCore as continuity contractor (non-voting), and not one street, because a council body cannot seat a person and the lowland is not drawn.**

**Nora Bell, at twenty past three, is asked the second question — the fourth asking — and answers with a number nobody asked her for: nine hundred and forty, nineteen years of pencil marks in a book, the number of *times* somebody has stood at that barrier end and been unable to cross.** *It is not nine hundred and forty people. It is a few hundred people, more than once each, and the number of people is not on the page and I have never tried to work it out, because if I work it out then it is a number of people.* Raynor-Hale says where it came from, in the room, for the minutes. **At ten past four she takes the eleventh place, and at a quarter past four she hands it back, and asks for her name to be taken off the roll. Councillor Martyn Craddock, forty-four, refuses, and is unforgivable and correct: the roll is a list, the only tool a body with ninety thousand pounds and eleven places has, and if names come off the first time a person is uncomfortable then this becomes a room where people only put their names down when they are certain. He does not fill the place and does not write *vacant* and will not change a number in a public document on a Tuesday afternoon to make one woman comfortable. Her words go in the minute unedited.**

**Ending, on the steps and in the stairwell:** Jonas asks one question — *is her name going in the quarterly* — and Raynor-Hale says yes, immediately, and that he has known since March that he is not on the right side of it. Then Raynor-Hale asks **whether the west lane is on the body**, and Jonas answers truthfully, including that the issuer is a named person in a dispatch book and all of it is public. **And in answering he says, out loud, that the man whose it is is going to fight it for eighteen months on his own** — which he promised on 28 April never to say, and does not telephone Dev Singh about, because Dev said he would deny it and would not be lying. *He kept a promise in the exact way that breaks it.* And **the clause was already public:** it is in a file a resilience officer with a card has been in, and it will be in a report in September whether Jonas says a word or not. The only private thing in it is a shop steward's dignity and a dispatcher's pride.

### Chapter 0065 — The Return Is Public

**Wednesday 7 May into Thursday 8 May. The Old Ward porch, the shop, the Carrow Lane corridor.** **The mid-batch turn: Nina Calder declares the Return of a Deferred Node a public document on Wednesday morning, having decided on the Sunday.** Reason, on the record: a man at a trade association rang on Tuesday for a copy of line three and said *I have it off a photocopy of the board, the whole page, including your crest.* **A form with a blank line at the bottom is honest. A form no party is required to answer is also a form four parties can decline to answer for as long as they like, and one a trade body can hold a copy of, and one that gets a photocopy passed round a market at six in the morning. From today the return is a public document — form number, crest, obtainable at the records office — and it is not adopted and it is not enforceable and it still establishes nothing, and every party holding maintenance is on notice that a copy of what they wrote is a copy of a public document. That is the only reason any of the nine will ever answer it properly.** *I have never in nineteen years made a thing public that a private person had written in their own hand.*

**The cost, told by Marguerite Oyelaran in a corridor at half past ten on the Thursday, plate having just printed the batch's second notice: `A RETURN MADE BY A PARTY HOLDING MAINTENANCE IS EVIDENCE OF A STATE. IT IS NOT EVIDENCE OF A PERSON, A HOUSEHOLD, OR A NUMBER.`** One: a four-page circular from the borough association of housing officers whose paragraph four says a chief officer is the answering party, the fee is recoverable only from rents, and officers in that position should take independent advice before 19 May, with a one-page letter signed by two people she has worked with for eleven years ending *we do not consider that a fee recoverable from rents can be defended in a public document without an answer to a question we have not been able to ask.* Two: a councillor's office has asked **whether the association should be in the room at all.** Three: **she is going into the Methodist hall on this road at two o'clock, where eleven households have been living at her expense since 17 April, and she is not going to be able to go**, because the telephone runs until three and then a room with two officers of an association until five, and a woman who rang on Monday is waiting for a call. *I am not going to be able to call her back until Monday, and in three weeks I will say the fee does not stop, and it will not be true on Monday, because I will not have been in that hall.*

**Nell Ardent, at the bench, refusing to give him the sentence in advance.** *I am going to stand in a gallery on Monday and say one sentence: the committee is asked to note that the only instrument in this city capable of producing the number it has been asked about was printed on the thirtieth of April and has no adoption behind it. And then I am going to sit down. And I am not going to give you the sentence so that you can do anything with it.* **And the thing she actually says: her form is how the plate got falsified inside four days by nobody doing anything wrong — every word of the circular and the councillor's question is true and is being used to stop a thing that was correct.**

**The council chamber, Wednesday evening, item 6, four minutes, nobody from the hearing turns up.** A member from Salt Row, the only prepared notes in the room, puts the question on the record and it is minuted unanswered: **should the Carrow Lane Housing Association be a party to the hearing on Monday at all — because if it is not, the only line in that room that says what a tier costs a person who lives in the block will be read out by a chair from a piece of paper a man off a register brought in at ten to eight in the morning, and that man is not a party either, and I do not know what that is.**

**Thursday, 8 May, twenty past nine: a queue of eleven in a corridor with two rooms and a landing, and there was no queue in January except Saturdays.** Jonas says eleven times: *she is the chief officer and she is through there, she is on the telephone.* He watches a circular move through eleven people in about an hour and a quarter: *four thousand two hundred pounds a node.* **The twelfth letter comes hand-delivered at ten past eleven, face down, by the woman from Block B room eleven, who counted on Saturday morning at breakfast and has told her daughter, and who would not put it in a box.** And at ten past twelve a man of about forty comes up from the bottom of the stairs, one of the three who did not understand it and whom nobody rang a fourth time, and says: *I understand it now. I understand that my block is on a piece of paper in a public building. I would like to be inside it. I am asking her, not you.* **Marguerite Oyelaran signs his name onto a page in a room at the end of the corridor and is sick about it, and Jonas says nothing, because he has promised to say one sentence and has said it eleven times and there is nothing else he is entitled to add.**

### Chapter 0066 — Nine Hundred Pounds

**Thursday 8 May into Friday 9 May. A corridor at Saint Orra west at ten past four, then the service manager's office on Friday at twenty to one.** **Jonas's goal is nothing.** For five weeks he has gone into every room with a thing he wanted; he has a card with £11,280 on it and *do not spend it before the nineteenth* and there is nothing else on any list he is carrying, and he spends Thursday in a yard doing a filing job that does not need doing. *She wrote come on foot and he understood it meant she did not want to see a car outside a hospital at ten past four for reasons she had not written down.*

**The mechanism of the chapter: a bench steriliser in the decontamination room on level zero, four years old, working; its supplier's service contract expired at midnight on 30 April; £1,100 for the year; an activity, not capital; due in the activity budget by five o'clock Friday or the room goes on a public compliance report in June. The activity budget had £913 in a dead 2011 coffee line until 22 April, when Halvorsen committed all of it to Priya Ranganathan's part-time on-call line. £1,100 out of pot two is the contract or the second clinician line, and it is both or neither. And the £4,200 cannot help: a continuity fee buys a node, and a supplier's maintenance contract is not a node. I have told it to two people and they both said *yes* and neither repeated it, and that is how I knew it was true.*** Both are correct. Signing a clinical declaration keeping the room open is correct; not signing is also correct; and if she signs, the money never comes and in November somebody reads the report and finds that the money was there on 8 May and went to a second on-call clinician instead.

**Sanaa refuses to sign, refuses to refuse, and refuses to look for a third thing, and does the second boring thing:** telephones the supplier as a physician and not a buyer and asks what they would do for less. **£410 — a quarterly service and a logged weekly chamber temperature verification, twenty minutes a week, the log going to the governance file.** *And I want you to hear the last part of that sentence properly, because I have said it out loud twice today and both times it sounded better than it is. Twenty minutes a week. On a corridor. In a building where the handover is two people at ten at night and there is a gap on the board from eleven to half past one every weekday, and where one man has refused a Thursday and asked for the refusal to be left where it is.* **She will not be thanked, in advance, in a corridor, in daylight, and she stops him saying *noted*:** *You are not allowed to write that down and put it in a coat. In four weeks there is going to be a review of this clinic's own witness structure, chaired by me, because I signed the renewal clause that needs two people in a corridor at ten at night, and I am going to have to sit in that room as the doctor who was in it, and one of the things I am not going to do is arrive with a piece of paper that a man off a register has been carrying since April explaining how good I am about money.* **Her one sentence about June, and it is not a request: THE NINTH OF JUNE. Ten in the morning. Not the committee, not CivicCore, not a hearing — a review of the clinic's own witness structure, chaired by her because she signed the clause that broke for forty hours, four of the nine people in the room on that roster, and he is not on it and there is nothing on it for him.**

**Halvorsen, Friday, three hours and ten minutes, a letter to the education office with the top torn off and unsent.** He is going to take the gate. The £410 is signed. The letter — a part-time on-call line suspended from **1 June** — goes on his desk at nine on Monday and he will tell her in person at nine and will not telephone her tonight. The twenty minutes a week goes into a rota with nobody against it, and he has not asked and is not going to. *I have not stopped her. I have not told her. And if I had told her she would have said no to both of us and been right about both.* **The fourth time in five weeks he has asked a man on that site a money question, the man has declined, and he has never had that from a technician in nineteen years.**

### Chapter 0067 — What Two Clinicians Cost

**Thursday 15 May, ten o'clock at night, the Saint Orra west corridor.** Jonas is there to watch a mending he is not allowed to make. **The handover now has two named clinicians, and for the first time in five weeks the door is opened by a person who is neither Ruth Nwosu nor Bram Osei nor Ezra Mbeki nor a declaration:** Priya Ranganathan, forty-one, part-time, on the roster since 2 May, not rostered for a Thursday night, who came because she asked Mina Okafor on Monday whether she could sit in on a handover and was told to come if she was going to. At ten past ten she declares the ninth emergency opening of the month in about seventy seconds, for a woman of eighty-one with a bed and a receiving shift. **She reads the five lines back unasked, gets one wrong, is corrected by Ezra Mbeki, and takes it.** And she refuses to be thanked: *being thanked for turning up to the witness requirement of your own job is a kind of thanks that stops a person coming back.* Jonas says he was not going to thank her and it is the first time in five weeks he has managed not to.

**Her question, and it is the one nobody has asked: the plate carries a date.** Two dates — the six-day window is 07:00 to 06:00 six days later, and the schedule expires 06:00 on Sunday 12 October. **The handover on Saturday 4 October read a term running 07:00 Sunday 5 October to 06:00 Sunday 12 October, which ends at the exact minute the schedule expires, to the minute, and is the last term in that building that exists entirely inside a funded schedule. The renewal is due on Saturday 11 October and a term read then begins 07:00 on the twelfth — one hour after the money stops. So the 11 October handover IS the crossing, it is a Saturday night, and no field on the plate says so.** The schedule is a cliff, the term is a cliff inside it, and there is not one field on that plate that could hold a scale, because nobody writes a scale between two cliffs. The plate's standing rule settles the rest: the node serves the term it was last read into. And if the 11 October renewal does not happen, on 12 October the door is on nothing at all, not on a reduced term. Jonas has the word — *a declining reduction* — and does not say more than that, because he is not on the rota, not a clinician, not a holder, not a witness, not a steward, and a stage-2 mending needs a person with local standing to instruct it and a named holder to take it, and there are four people in that corridor and not one of them is asking him for anything. **She works out the rest herself, standing at the plate, in order: on 12 October at 06:00 this node is not on a schedule, so it is deferred, so it serves its last verified essential use, of which there is none, so it serves the node's maintenance — and the maintenance on a care entrance is a rota, and there is no rota, so it serves itself, so there is nothing on the plate but the engraving and a date in 2014, and what is left is a fallback that names a route and not a man, and a route is a door and a door is not a person.** She refuses to be instructed, refused in three reasons — the being-on-a-rota problem, the plate will not take a bare role and she has a daughter of eleven, and she will not be thanked — and then gives the answer nobody wanted: **nobody is going to offer. She does two days a week. Ruth will not, she has a red diary in her bag. Ezra will not, he is twenty-six and has done the two-to-ten shift since 2 April as well as the handover. Bram is going to refuse, for the reason he gave in March, and she is not going to be the person who asks him for a second refusal in October so that there are two on a board.**

**And the oldest question in the building gets a date — SATURDAY 11 OCTOBER, the handover, not the Sunday: if two of the four say no there is no rule, there has never been a rule, it has been asked five times and never answered, and every person who could answer it is on the rota and cannot answer it without being on it.** She says: *write it down and do not do anything with it — that is a sentence I have said to one other person in five weeks and I am saying it to you because you are the only one who has obeyed it.* **Tomas Renn takes it at eleven o'clock from a stairwell:** *read it back to me, the one you asked in March. — What happens when two people on the same rota both refuse. — That has got a date on it now.* He has never been given a date in four months. **He is going to ask it in the shop on Monday the nineteenth, in front of about nine people, not bringing Jonas in, Ada will vote against him, and then he is going to go and stand in a corridor in Saint Orra west on Thursday the twenty-second of May and say the date out loud to the four of them. And he tells Jonas not to come into the site on the twenty-second or the twenty-ninth or any day between, and if he needs him he will ask in a way he can refuse.** *The fact that you have a fourth question with a date on it and no plan attached to it is the first honest thing anybody has handed me since the eighteenth of April, and I am not going to thank you for it, and I would like it noted that I noticed.*

### Chapter 0068 — The First Transfer That Works

**Saturday 17 May from seven in the morning to Sunday 18 May, eight in the evening. The lowland chamber, a room in the civic offices, and a garden in Tideglass.** The plate in the chamber four hundred metres short of the dip: **6-L/16 — LOWLAND SPUR AND OUTFALL, Salt Row boundary to the dip, 480 m, no interruption.** Holder empty, its name gone. **Tier 1.** Not deferred — *somebody with a standing continuity authorisation paid £1,880 for this spur and put it on a schedule on Tuesday the twelfth of May, signed with a power of attorney and not with a name, and the party holding that authorisation is the company that maintains the gauge*, because the gauge is what told Emeka Rusk at eleven minutes past midnight on 29 April that the lowland was going. He has been sitting on it since Wednesday and did not believe it until he read the fee stamp.

**The batch's third notice, on the plate: `A NODE THAT IS ON A SCHEDULE AT A TIER IS NOT HELD BY THE PARTY THAT PAYS. IT IS HELD BY THE PARTY THAT ANSWERS. A PARTY THAT ANSWERS MAY BE ASKED WHAT IT WILL DO AND NOT WHAT IT WILL COST.`** The money is the company's; the holder is the duty engineer for the district, who is nineteen minutes away and said no in forty seconds in the dark on 30 April and has not been in that chamber since the 22nd. **And the thing that fell: the outfall inspection duty — go to the outfall when the supply is restricted or the gauge drops, look at the screens and the chamber, enter a fault, stand there while the restriction is on — has had no holder since 20:00 on 29 April, because it was held on this node and the term broke for six hours and ten minutes. There is no such thing as a lapsed duty. A duty that is not held is a duty that is being done by a man who is not on it and is not paid for it, and he put the last fault in with the operator box blank.**

**The insight, and it is Jonas's and it is a real one: a duty is not a place.** Pell's 30 April ruling was that describing the *water point* would need a location of record, and a location of record would be a survey, and a survey is a count, and a count is the price. **An outfall is not a water point. It is an act, and an act can be held by a man at a desk in Gantry Ward with a finding in his name, and the finding does not put the lowland in a book.** Emeka Rusk's two objections are both correct: whoever holds it does the work or pays for it, and the last time somebody held a duty on a lowland structure unpaid it was a road sweeper who is alive; and if you ask a man and he says no, you have told a man in a register you have been thinking of giving him something. *Ask him, and I am not going to be in the room.*

**The transfer, in a room on the second floor of the civic offices, typed and read back and initialled before it was typed, and Nina Calder's six items:** claim 6-L/16, basis, duty described as an act and not a place, **no location of record created, no address, household, or person recorded**; moved by **MERCER, J.**, civic standing, register of attachment, released by no person; held by **ACHEBE, R.**, interim lowland maintenance post, in his own name; term to 06:00 on the last day of the parent node's fee year, no carry-forward, renewable by the holder only; withdrawn by —; claim unpaid, and the party that pays for the spur does not answer for it. **Witness E. RUSK. Second witness: NIL** — not an oversight, printed because it is the truth. *That is the first record in nineteen years with *moved by* on it, and the last two lines are going to be read in a room by somebody who is angry, and the second one is the true one and the first one is the reason anybody will be angry, and I have put them in that order on purpose.* **Achebe requires the cost in the terms: *moved by* in his own words, so that in August he can point at a piece of paper and not at a memory.** **Nina Calder refuses the word register:** a register is a list, and she has spent a week in May learning what a list does to a hundred and twenty-eight people in four blocks; this is an entry on a file that already exists, and there will be forty of them in nine years and by then it will be a list and by then she will not be here.

**The reading, in a garden in Tideglass at eight on Sunday evening with the last of the light on a lemon tree in a pot, per the registrar's rule — in daylight, in the place where that person keeps what they are holding.** Emeka Rusk reads the claim in his own words. Achebe reads the six items back slowly, gets one wrong, and is corrected. **The rule Achebe cannot get out of:** a holder may offer a held claim back once, in writing, within seven days of the reading; the offer is recorded; **the offer does not transfer the claim;** the claim is released only when a successor consents; **and a person who has not been asked may not be named.** So the only door out is to make somebody findable, and he will not do that to a man he met once at a bus stop, or to Emeka Rusk, or to the duty engineer. He offers it back inside the first hour and does not use a minute of the rest.

**And the sentence that is not the one from April: *I would rather have had it on the twenty-ninth of April. In April I was refusing a thing. Refusing is what a man does. This week I am asking to give one back, and asking is what a man does, and asking puts a date and a name and a shape on me, and there is nobody in this city who is on more shapes than I am, and every one of the shapes so far has had somebody else's name in it, and this is the first one where the shape is me and the name is also me.*** He picks up the watering can.

### Chapter 0069 — Six Points and One Number

**Sunday 18 May, the room above the shop at about eight in the evening, then the embankment, then Monday 19 May from a quarter past seven in the morning.** He writes the number out twice and the second one is any good. **He does not put it in a card, a claim, or a speech, and the claims box is four hundred yards away and closes on Friday 18 July, and a claim there would have established nothing and would have established nothing and put £11,280 and a district nobody can name into a public document ninety days before a hearing in which a contractor is asking the city to adopt a price list.** He has been told twice that the count is the clipboard. **So he is going to put a number in the record before he says it out loud, and make sure that when he says it he is reading a document that had existed since 28 April, written in another person's hand, on another person's wall, and his only contribution is the reading.** The sheet: source (Carrow Lane corridor board, Marsh Lane, the hand of M. Oyelaran, chief officer, Monday 28 April about ten past eleven, in the presence of the undersigned); the four lines quoted exactly; and four statements — **he does not ask any party to place the six nodes on a schedule, he asks that the figure be in the record; the six nodes have no number, no location of record, no district, no holder and no recorded date of deferral and nothing in the paper supplies any of them and he accepts the registrar's ruling and has not tried to work around it; no person, household, occupancy figure or list is asserted and none may be derived; he is not a party, not a member, not an officer, not a submitter, and is in the gallery.** He crosses out the word *lowland* on the fourth reading and writes it back, because the chief officer wrote it and he is not going to improve her.

**The flaw, at half past ten on Sunday night, in a car on the embankment with the engine off.** He does not go up the path. He keeps the promise as written and commits the thing it was about: one of the six lowland points is the stand at the top of that slipway, and Margo Doré has said she wants the street left with nothing on it because a pump is a thing and a thing can be bought, and in ten hours he is going to stand in a gallery and put a Tier 1 price on her water **without asking her**, because the number had a date on it and was worth nothing in nine days and worth something on the nineteenth. She had asked him for one thing, on 21 April, and had told him not to come up the path at two in the morning without something that cannot wait. *Did not go up. Did not telephone. Said it again on Monday in a room and she is not in it, and she is the only person in this city who asked me for a sentence and I have never once told her I was going to use it in one. That is the third time I have kept a promise in the exact way that breaks it.*

**07:10 Monday, outside committee room two.** Frances Tolley, twenty-two years, will not let him put it under the door. *You are not on the agenda. You are not a party. You are not a departmental officer. Give it to me and I will give it to her, and I will tell you now that she will not read it to the Committee.* She also tells him the gallery fills at about eleven and the front two rows go to a press list that nobody checks at the door. **Marsh takes it at ten to eight and reads all fifty seconds of it standing in a corridor and asks where the figure came from, and is told that he wrote no confession into a document that goes into a minute. It should not be on the sheet. It is a bad habit and you have it from a union submission.** And what she will do: *I am going to read it out in full at the point in the sitting where the return is taken, and I will not comment on it, and it will be in the minute as a document received. I cannot make anybody do anything about eleven thousand pounds. I can make sure that the sentence exists in a place where it can be read out.* **At eleven minutes past eleven she reads the four lines and the four statements and says "Thank you" and goes on to the next thing, and the clerk types it, and that was the whole of it.**

### Chapter 0070 — The Nineteenth of May

**Monday 19 May, 10:00 to about 16:10. Committee room two.** Marsh in the chair; Sore, Imre, Hargreaves, Okonjo, Denman. **Pell in attendance with a coat over the back of the seventh chair. Rook and Vaughan for CivicCore, and a third man with a lanyard who is not introduced. The gallery is full at ten to eleven — forty-one folding chairs and about thirty standing at the rail.** Jonas is fourth seat back on the left, on the advice of a clerk of twenty-two years. **He looks for Tomas Renn in the first recess for four minutes, does not find him, knows he is not there, does not telephone, and had promised on 27 April not to be.** Marguerite Oyelaran is in the back row on the left, a party to nothing, having said nothing all day to eleven people who wanted to talk to her. A member from Marsh Lane raises that **the covering submission asks the committee to approve and adopt while the tabling list says referred on**; the chair will not rule, the point is put, and it takes eleven minutes, and the Committee proceeds on the document in front of it. **Petrie Okonjo asks that the minute record that the author of the document the contractor relies on is not in the room, and the chair says the clerk minuted it before she asked.**

**Nobody succeeds at beating a price with a sentence. It is attempted four times in three hours and it fails four times, and every failure is correct.** Rook does not define *holder* — he never will — and makes the volume's best argument: *the schedule does not price access to urgent care, it prices the continuity of a node; a care claim is not a node; the west clinic care entrance is not in failure and is not on a schedule and Schedule 4 did not touch it, and that door has been open every night since 2 April on a term read aloud at ten o'clock by two people who can say when it ends, and that is the only thing anybody in this city has done right since 3 April.* **The chair does not stop anybody, exactly as Alden Rook predicted on 30 April** — and then a member asks whether the contractor's own glossary supplies a definition, and Ceri Vaughan, continuity counsel, offers **page 460, sub-paragraph 3, which has been there since 1 March**, in the spirit of not wasting anybody's time, and it is a good offer. **Pell leans forward an inch and says, in eleven seconds, to the chair and not the room: a definition given in the course of considering a document is a determination of that document; the Committee has not determined to determine Schedule 4; and if a definition is minuted today the Committee will have adopted a four-hundred-and-sixty-page schedule without a division and nobody will have voted on it.** Marsh says it is correct, says stopping it is not the chair's job and that she is doing it anyway, and tells Imre what happens if he moves it. **Imre withdraws and asks that the withdrawal be minuted with the reason — the registrar's advice, which is good, and he will not have it become a rumour in six weeks that the Committee declined out of cowardice.** At twenty past one, on a motion of the chair agreed without a division, **the Committee records that it is unable to define the term *holder*,** in Imre's words, and will report on the definition at its next sitting if one is before it.

**The chair's two determinations at ten past three, and they are the only reason anybody reads the transcript in September.** One: **the Return of a Deferred Node is received into the records of the Committee — not adopted, received.** Two: **every party holding continuity in this city is required to answer a Return of a Deferred Node, in public, on the form circulated with the papers, by the next sitting, and the answers are to be read into the record as they are received; and where the answer to *who answers for it* is no party, that is to be recorded as no party, in those words, and read into the record as no party.** Her reason, in order: a node with no answering party is a fact about a node and not a fact about a person; this city has no instrument that can record a fact about a node; it has four hundred and sixty pages of price list; and at some point somebody is going to have to be the person who starts the record, and it is her, and in four years the chair will be somebody else who will not know why. **And the operative sentence, which is the batch's third notice said out loud in a room by a person who has never read the plate it came from: a party that answers may be asked what it will do, and not what it will cost. A tier may not be challenged by a person. That is in the document, at page nine, and it is the best sentence in it, and it has been read at me in a meter room in this borough four times this month by four different people, and I have now read it out in a Committee room and I have discovered that when you say it in here it does something. It does not make anybody cheaper. It makes a claim answerable.**

**Nine minutes past eleven of the morning, before any of it:** the notice on the Old Ward board — `WHERE TWO TERMS APPLY TO ONE USE AND NEITHER HAS PRECEDENCE, THE USE IS PERFORMED AND ENTERED, AND NOBODY IS BLAMED, AND THE ENTRY IS MADE TWICE` — with CivicCore's own return pinned over line three, answering for the same meter room the borough's return answers for, and no precedence between them, and both entries standing. **Nineteen minutes past two the ninth line of the first return is read and nobody fills the gap. The chair says the word in the room and not in the minute, because a chair should not put a word into a minute that a records supervisor has not put into a form — and Nina Calder, from the second row of the public seats, says the word is *nobody*, that she has had the form since 20 April, that she has been waiting nineteen days for a chair to ask her, and that she would like to write it tomorrow in her own words.** The third thing in the record, and the word itself is written on 20 May.

**Sore moves at two twenty-five and it is not the thing anybody expected, in four sentences, and she does not explain it:** the Committee is asked to consider a document whose entire operative content is a price list; **a Committee that is unable to define the word *holder* is entirely capable of counting what has been paid for a schedule**; I am not asking this Committee to like the number, I am asking it to be in the minute; a Committee that will not do the arithmetic of a document it has been asked to uphold is not a Committee. There is no figure. CivicCore states four, including the Tier 1 fee of £1,880 for a lowland spur placed on a schedule on 12 May, in the room, in a corridor, in front of a chamber, for a fortnight. The borough holds none and cannot say what it does not hold. **Marguerite Oyelaran, from the public seats, states sixteen thousand eight hundred pounds for four nodes and the chair makes her say it twice and she asks for it to be minuted exactly as she said it both times.** The motion goes in as an outstanding request. It is the only motion all afternoon that the gallery applauded, and the chair says *the gallery will refrain, please, this is a Committee and not a public meeting.* **At twenty to three Nell Ardent is allowed, without motion or notice, as a point of order about the papers, to say one sentence — the only instrument in this city capable of producing the number it has been asked about was printed on 30 April and has no adoption behind it — and the chair notes it and says the reason she allowed it is that it is a sentence about the Committee's own papers and because she noticed, in a chair, for six hours, who was going to ask the first question in this city about a price list, and it was never going to be her.**

**Jonas's one question, asked at ten to four, from the fourth seat back.** *Is the Committee going to require somebody to answer for the six?* The chair will not take a question from him at first because he has not asked one; then she takes one, because the document is on the table. **Her answer is no, and the reason is page nine: *the Committee cannot require a person. So the answer to your question is no, and the reason the answer is no is the same reason nobody has asked her.*** A party that answers may be asked what it will do and not what it will cost. The six nodes have no party that answers for them, **and that is not a mystery and it is not a scandal and it is not anybody's failure. It is the position of a thing that nobody maintains and nobody has priced and nobody can be found at, in a city where being found at is the price.** She will not make a person at six water points on a lowland and wants it minuted that she was asked to and declined in the only words available. **What she will do is smaller: in September there will be a public, minuted record of a number of nodes in this city that have no party answering for them, the number will be more than one, and the record will be a record of a state and not of a person, and there will not be a single name on it, and no address, no survey, and no count of anything that lives anywhere.** And then the whole of the hearing in one sentence: **the next document anybody makes about those points will have to argue with that sentence, and the sentence will be there, and it will have a date on it, and it will have been read out in a room with forty-one people in it and put in a minute by a clerk who has been doing it for twenty-two years and who did not have to be asked.**

**The three things the city puts into the record: a registrar's record of 18 April; a form with a hole in it, received; and a return with an unanswered ninth line and the word *nobody* waiting in Nina Calder's hand to be written on 20 May.**

**Marguerite Oyelaran on the steps, at half past four, tells him the fourth statement is the only sentence anybody has said to her in six weeks she did not have to work out the second half of on her own, that she is not withdrawing her objection, that on Thursday morning she signed a man's name onto a page because his block is on a photocopy and she would have done the wrong thing too — and that on Wednesday a councillor asked whether her association should be in the room at all, and it was not about her, it was about the fact that a housing association is the only kind of body in this city that has one person's money inside another person's rent and no instrument anywhere that anybody can see. Four hundred and sixty pounds of somebody's money in a person's rent was the phrase she used and it was the wrong phrase and she let it stand. She gets in a car and drives off with her own money, which she has done since 2011.**

**The porch, at about ten past five, in the sun, on his own, and the board:** the root field unchanged, the returns in their acrylic, the claims box with one card in it about a stair, the new notice about two terms and one use. And pinned to the frame, a **Borough Heat Threshold Protocol, activation on three consecutive days at 27°C or above, requiring residential care premises to confirm occupancy and a named responsible person for every address in their custody, with a telephone number.** It had been twenty-nine on the Old Ward bridge at four o'clock. The next four days were forecast at thirty, thirty-one, twenty-nine and twenty-eight. The spring tides are the 21st, 22nd, 28th, 29th and 1 June. And in the pad, four lines, the last he writes in May:

> *It is not defeated. It is referred on. It is going to cost somebody a lift in a block and a gate at a hospital and a handrail on a stair on a Sunday in October, and it is going to cost all three of those to people who have not got a vote.*
> *A city can keep a service floor. That is the half I still believe and I cannot prove and neither can they. The other half is the price of the floor when somebody cannot pay it, and nobody in this city has yet had to answer that question about a person, because a person cannot be challenged and a tier can, and every single thing that happened in that room today was arranged around that one sentence, and the sentence is on page nine, and it was written by the contractor as a protection, and it has worked.*
> *So the question, in the only words I have got: can a city keep a shared service floor when urgent access is legally priced — and when a resident refuses the price, who pays for the refusal?*
> *Not the association. Not the contractor. Not the city. Somebody in that block, on the first of June, in a heat, in a room with a fixed stopcock in a cupboard nobody has a key to.*


## Rolling Handoff 6 — VOLUME 02 OPEN (SUPERSEDED BY HANDOFF 7)

The city has found out that a word it could not define can be supplied by whoever hands it a schedule. A document it has had since the first of March has turned a twenty-four-hour maintenance guarantee into a market, and the market's central fact — that a node which stops being paid for does not go dark, it goes **thin** — was printed on a cast plate in a mortuary yard and is now the most useful sentence anybody in Nacre has read all year. **A deferred node narrows to its last verified essential use, and if none is recorded, it serves its own maintenance.** At Saint Orra west the maintenance on a mortuary gate is a man with a key, and a man of twenty-nine with a laundry bag wrote one down in nine seconds on the back of a delivery note, and it is the first hollow term in this city in nineteen years, and a registrar has declined to strike it and has said out loud that there are about nine hundred and nobody counts them.

**Rule 4(c): a signature on a participation agreement is a consent mark and may not be withdrawn except on the same date next year.** Nineteen of thirty-one signed at Wren Court, Salter's Yard, in daylight with the numbers read out four times. Four have missed a month. Eleven people were in that building on the night of the seventeenth and all eleven were on a lapsed term and the water was at half, and the steward has not told them. **Separately, and never to be run together: one hundred and twenty-two of a hundred and twenty-eight signed at the Carrow Lane Housing Association, nine have missed a month, and eleven of the hundred and twenty-two in Block C were on a lapsed term on 18 April with the water at half. A signature is worth three hundred and seventy-five days and after that it is worse than nothing, and there is no appeal against a date. Marguerite Oyelaran is losing Block C and has said she is going to lose it.**

**Six lowland water points. £1,880 each. £11,280 a year. Nobody has asked.** The number is on a housing association's corridor board in a chief officer's own handwriting because a technician did not use it.

A man in a good coat sat in a car for forty-five minutes at one o'clock in the morning and offered to buy the lowland for twenty-five thousand two hundred pounds in eleven minutes, said it was a purchase and not a favour, and drove away without waiting to be answered. **They did not buy it.** Six hours and ten minutes, six bottles, a hundred and nine steps, and nobody wrote it down. **The Weir's silt trap can no longer be cleared by anybody, ever, and the bank is gone, and Margo Doré has said out loud that she wants the street left with nothing on it, and a pump has not been offered yet.**

The next batch is **Chapters 60–70**, and it must open on or before **Monday 19 May**.

### Chapter 0071 — One Station, Four Miles Away

**Tuesday 20 May into Wednesday 21 May. The Old Ward porch, the ground-floor canteen of the civic offices, the basement print room, and two rooms above a betting shop.** Jonas's goal is to find out what the Borough Heat Threshold Protocol asks for and what it measures before it measures anything. He reads the small line he had not read on the Monday: **the threshold is measured at the borough's designated station, the Saltmarsh Station, Fourteen Dock Road**, which is four miles out, the only station in the borough with a continuous daily record since 1974, and the reason there is one threshold and not a moving one. **Gareth Raynor-Hale gives him the definition of *residential care premise* — the 2009 heatwave wording, and the 2009 wording was written after the Ravenscourt inquest — and tells him the whole of how the Carrow Lane Housing Association got inside it: the licensing register carries every residential address anybody has ever registered for anything, the association is on it because of a gas inspection in 2011, and every line of the email is correct and he has not shown it to anybody because there is nobody to show it to.** Raynor-Hale has taken about forty readings since Saturday with his mother's thermometer at four o'clock in a car park, and the bridge and a betting shop on Carrow Lane, and there is nowhere to put them, and that is the finding. **His prediction, and it is the chapter's clock: the borough is on its second day, and if the saltmarsh holds it across the weekend then the protocol activates on Monday morning, and the thing it activates is a form. It does hold, and it activates on Monday 26 May at 09:00, and it is the third consecutive day, and he adds that the saltmarsh has been under on eleven days in May and over on eleven and the middle days are the ones that decide this city.** **The basement printer, Ellis Fenn, thirty-one, in a print cardigan, said the true thing about the drawing layer for the fourth time in six weeks: the layer will draw you a shape and a term and a date and the claim under it and it has never once drawn what a thing stopped doing — and to know the temperature on the lowland you have to stand on it.** The porch, the enamel strip, no notice in this chapter. **Marguerite Oyelaran has had the protocol face up on her desk since Monday morning and has not rung anybody, and gives the reason: she has been waiting eighteen days, from the second of May, to find out whether anybody in this city would ask her a question before asking her a question about her, and on 19 May a chair read out a number she had written on a wall in this room, and she was in the gallery and heard it and has not slept, and if he had asked her she would have said no in eleven words and it would not have been in a minute.** She has not been asked one single question about anything since the second of May and everybody has been extremely careful not to ask her, and she has noticed, and it is the first nice thing anybody has done for her since March.

### Chapter 0072 — Block C Has No Key

**Wednesday 21 May into Friday 23 May. A meter room, a corridor, a back yard at half past one in the afternoon.** **The stopcock is reachable and has always been reachable. There is no key and there has never been a key, because the cupboard has a hasp and no lock and has never had one, and the brass tag says STOPCOCK / 1998.** The plate on that wall now has four lines, and the new one is the batch's first notice: `WHERE A SERVICE IS NARROWED FOR A REASON OF SEASON, THE NARROWING IS PERFORMED AND ENTERED, AND THE ENTRY IS MADE IN THE FORM AND IN THE LOG, AND THE TWO ARE NOT REQUIRED TO AGREE.` The association's own log's last entry is *Stopcock found closed. No action. Unable to locate stopcock key. Housing Association informed 29/4.* The block's return line reads: state NO SUPPLY, since 06/05, who answers M. OYELARAN, CHIEF OFFICER. **Marguerite Oyelaran refuses to have any of it looked at, and the reason is the shape of the volume: the day she looks at it is the day it is a record, and a crest on a wall is the beginning of a circular, and she has had two of those in eleven years.** **Nadine Prentice, 29, the freeholder's agent's clerk, fourteen months in post, came about the meters and wrote `stopcock accessible, no key, tag reads 1998` in her own file unbidden and said she will not be thanked and will not be blamed because the second is more likely.** **The internal riser in Block C died in February 1998 and was never replaced, and eight households have carried the water for thirty rooms in buckets ever since, and it has never been in a letter.
