Ivy Marsden, seventy-two, asks for one letter saying the buckets are theirs, and has been refused twice in sixteen years.** She has walked from the top landing of a block with no water, twice on Tuesday, since 6 May. **And Marguerite Oyelaran says, in the yard, why she has not stopped the £2.60 on those eight households and will not: refunding them would mean admitting in writing that a service in one of her four blocks has stopped, and a service that stops is a node that goes into failure, and a node in failure is a thing this city has a document about — she would rather be hated for it than find out in August what she has done by stopping it.** Ivy Marsden: that is the first straight answer I have had about that money. **And the thing nobody said out loud, which Jonas only understands on the Friday: the eight households who carry the water are the only people in that block who have a reason to want the schedule to keep working, because the Tier 2 keeps the node funded and the node's maintenance is a meter, a stopcock and a cupboard — and they are not on the agreement, they did not get a form, and a tier may not be challenged by a person.** Her sentence, carried since 28 April and said at last on Friday morning: **I do not know which of the two I am more afraid of. I am afraid of the four thousand three hundred and sixty-one pounds and sixty, because in November there is no money and four blocks have no water. And I am more afraid of the other one, which is that in two years this association is a number, and a number is the only thing anybody can be measured on, and I did the measuring.**

### Chapter 0073 — Four People and a Sunday

**Thursday 22 May and Friday 23 May. The shop, a telephone, a bench.** **Jonas does not go into the west site on the twenty-second, and the day is spent on a cabinet in a corner that has had a fault since February, which he fixes properly, and the tide table, and nothing in particular.** Bea Osunde, from the bench, before the day starts: *he will have done it in a corridor and it will have taken six minutes and it will have changed nothing, and you will not find out until tomorrow.* **He learns the shape afterwards, at 16:30, by telephone, from MINA OKAFOR, who was at the card table and was not in the corridor and says so first, and gives ninety seconds and one fact: at 12:10 Ruth Nwosu put an A5 on the card table with the word `unscheduled` in capitals and nothing else on it, and said, if you see a man with a date on the back of his hand, put it in the box, and do not put a name on it — and when Mina said *what date*, Ruth said that was hers to give and not hers to ask after.** It went in the box at 12:10 with who put it up and at what time and *I do not know where it came from*, and it is the only paper in that box in five weeks with no name on it. **Mina's rule: she will not put anything in the box she could not hear, and six minutes of four people saying nothing she could hear from the end of a corridor is not evidence of anything.** **On Friday morning Tomas Renn gives the number in the yard, and it is the whole of what he has: ELEVEN Thursday handovers from 6 March to 15 May, performed by a day porter who is not on the roster, unpaid, uncounted, out of a shop diary and a porter's wage book, in nine minutes, counted out loud in a corridor in front of all four of them on purpose, because in April the registrar said there are about nine hundred of these and nobody counts them, and the reason nobody counts them is that counting one is a man standing in a corridor going through a book.** **Every one of the four gave him a correct answer about what happens if two of them say no at the 22:00 handover on 11 October, and none of them answered the question, because they cannot answer it without being on it. Ezra asked him in front of the other three not to write it down. He wrote it down anyway and has sent it to nobody and is not going to, and the cost is that a twenty-six-year-old man now knows a shop steward has him in a diary.** His own reckoning, given in a doorway: the number and what the number costs are in one hand, and that is the first honest thing anybody has handed him since 18 April, and he is not going to thank anybody for it, and he would like it noted that he noticed. And the ending: **Priya asked on Friday night, from a kitchen with a child in the background, for a Sunday in October to go in front of the board in the same box as Bram's refused Thursday, in the same words, with her name and a date. Bram said she may not** — *you may not put your name next to mine, because the second one on that board is not a second refusal, it is a reason for the first one to be looked at, and in about four months somebody is going to use her to get me, and she has got a daughter of eleven* — and Tomas refused to put her name to it without her saying it twice, and **nothing has been put in front of the board.**

### Chapter 0074 — The First Transfer That Has To Be Repeated

**Saturday 24 May into Sunday 25 May. A lowland chamber, a slipway, a room in Gantry Ward, a works van doorway on the tide.** The gauge on 6-L/16 drops four-tenths in twenty minutes at 08:11, and the spur has a fee and a restoration route and a form because CivicCore paid £1,880 on 12 May with a power of attorney. The new plate line is the batch's second notice: `WHERE A DUTY IS HELD BY A PERSON AND THE PERSON IS NOT AT THE PLACE, THE DUTY DOES NOT MOVE. IT WAITS. AND A DUTY THAT WAITS IS STILL A DUTY AND STILL UNPAID.` **Achebe, who is the holder, is forty minutes away writing a report. He says I cannot send it, the offer does not transfer, the only door out is a successor who consents, so the duty waits, and I would be grateful if you did not do anything about it. His seven-day window expires on the 25th and he uses no minute of it.** Jonas looks, and **does not enter the fault, because entering a fault is an act by a person with standing and the person with standing is not there.** Emeka Rusk enters it in his own hand with the operator box left blank, for the twenty-sixth time since 2 April. **Jonas asks him to hold it and he says no, in three reasons that are all correct: if he holds it the £1,880 node is his and he cannot ring the company; he will not have Achebe handed a thing that has already been handed about once, and neither man asked him; and a duty cannot be half-shared, because if he holds it Achebe is out, and helping is what he has been doing, and helping does not go on a form and is why nobody counts it.** What he asks for instead is that the duty be recorded — and he does not want it on a form. **Pell's ruling, by telephone at 20:20, in eleven seconds: the record has no field for a duty that is held by one person and performed by another, and where a record has no field, the entry is made twice, and nobody is blamed.** The second entry is typed and read back: held by Achebe, holder not at the place, duty not moved; performed by Rusk, not a holder, not a witness to this entry; **and `moved by: MERCER, J.` on it a second time, at Nina Calder's insistence, because the two entries on that file are incompatible and the file will not know the difference and he will not be able to prove it is not true.** **The ending is Achebe's own account of it, said to him on the telephone late on the night of Saturday 24 May and written down by Jonas on a page at about half past eleven, in his own words and not the man's, and not in the shop book: in August somebody will ring Jonas Mercer about a Tuesday, and the man who will ring him is Royden Achebe, and he has got until then to work out whether he is going to be the one who answers, and Achebe has not got the right to be angry with him either way, because he asked him in a garden on the eighteenth for eleven hours of his life and he gave it, correctly, and the only thing left in this account that is Achebe's to do about it is to be the one who rings, and he is not going to pretend to anybody that he is calling about a screen. Jonas wrote *corridor* in the margin first and struck it out, because it was a garden, and because he was not going to get that wrong either.**

### Chapter 0075 — Three Consecutive Days

**Tuesday 26 May into Wednesday 27 May. A standpipe, a chamber, a service manager's office, a yard.** **The protocol activates at 09:00 on the third consecutive day. Four people in the city have rung the number; two of them were in the flood forum.** At the lowland standpipe at the end of Carrow Lane there is a forty-second cycle on a tap that ran freely last month, **because the borough narrowed the supply for the season: the log says the restriction lifts on 2 June at 06:00, the line on the form says 14 May at 06:00, nobody has lied, and the two are not required to agree.** Four people queue at half past ten. **Saint Orra west's estate water came up out of a chamber on the north side at 03:11 and did not come back. Node 6-51, tank feed, chamber, meter, two tanks on the roof, in the asset register since 1974, no fee, no tier, no restoration route. The borough crew came at 11:20 — eight hours — and the fault was a gland nut done up in a hurry in 2011 and a rag in a strainer, and the fault box said DEBRIS and Jonas's instruction afterwards was to write RAG, because debris means a design and there was no design. The node was restored at about 19:20 that evening, which is eight hours on site.** Between about 04:30 and 07:10 that morning the domestic water ran at about a fifth, the reduction was performed and entered correctly in two books, **and the two books agreed, for the first time in six weeks.** **THE BATCH'S CENTRAL BEAT, DRAMATISED AND NOT NARRATED: at about ten in the morning, in daylight, at a table, with a form and a crest on it, Gunnar Halvorsen spends £1,880 of an activity budget of £2,140 to put 6-51 on Tier 1 from 1 June, and he says twice that it is the only door in the room, and he is right and everybody in the building agrees he is right.** He gives the three alternatives without softening — a tanker at about £600 a load with no budget line, requisitioning that takes four days and a board that meets on Thursdays, and do nothing, which is Monday. **He says, unasked and in advance, that a continuity fee buys a node and is not an hour of anybody's time, and that if anybody in that building ever tells a member of staff this fee has paid for a gap in a rota then they are lying and he will deny having heard it. £260 is left. The gap is still a gap. Priya is still two days a week until Saturday the thirty-first. He did not ask Jonas for anything and said so twice, and offered him the witness line instead and got nothing, and told him not to sit in the chair and decide he has found the pattern in it.** **Bea Osunde gives him one of four sentences in a yard: a vacuum with your face in it is a hole, and a man with money in a room and a form on a table is not a hole, and there is not one thing in that sentence you could have done better — and I have watched you for four months and you have never once been in a position where doing nothing was the correct thing and I have not got a single useful thing to say about it.** He does not write the lesson on the page. **Every repair in that week is correct and the district still runs out of water: 6-51 restored with a stated time on a plate; Block C on its seventeenth day without a drop while £4,200 a year is paid for its node's continuity; six lowland points in their fifth week on a trickle, one of them the Weir stand, whose trap cannot be cleared by anybody ever, and which in thirty-one degrees is the only thing anybody on that street wants — a man said exactly that and Jonas did not offer anybody a pump and did not go up the path and did not telephone anybody.** The ending, on Wednesday afternoon in a corridor: **Halvorsen gives Jonas the date — Thursday the twenty-ninth, three days, the 11:00–13:30 gap out of £260 — and says he will not tell him which place he will not be in, and that Jonas can find out from the transcript in about nine days.**

### Chapter 0076 — A Form With a Crest On It

**Thursday 28 May into Friday 29 May. The Old Ward porch, a records office, two rooms above a betting shop, a telephone.** The batch's third notice prints overnight on the enamel strip: `A PARTY THAT HAS BEEN ASKED TO CONFIRM OCCUPANCY HAS BEEN ASKED FOR A STATE AND NOT FOR A LIST, AND A PARTY THAT ANSWERS WITH A LIST HAS ANSWED A DIFFERENT QUESTION.` **Nina Calder lays the two forms on a table with a straight edge between them and refuses the sentence he brings in: they are NOT the same instrument, and if he says that in a room in September he will be wrong in front of about nine people. They have exactly one thing in common, which is the absence of a power.** What makes it a fact: **the protocol's first box is a number box, with a line the width of a ruler and no ceiling on it, and a box with a line in it and no ceiling is the most dangerous object in the civic office, and she has watched two hundred men fill one in and eleven of them put *all* in it.** The word in her own hand on the ninth line of the 20 May return — the fourth return, Cooper's Reach clinic line — is on the table, dated, and nobody re-stages where it came from. **And the practical finding she has chewed on for nine days: for eight days this city has had a public obligation with no date on it. There is no next sitting. The obligation is discharged by the act of answering and not by anybody receiving it, nobody is in breach, and nobody will ever be told that they are not.** **Marguerite Oyelaran will not fill the form in: two boxes, a number and a hundred and twenty-eight names, and she has put a hundred and twenty-two consent marks through letterboxes already, and if she puts one name in that box she has put a name in a holder field, which she refused in nine seconds for six water points in May.** **The person who does it does it without her: DENISE HOYLE, 61, two days a week, six years, not an officer, never been in a room, who opens about four hundred plastic wallets a year and is extremely good at what is in them, and who at about twenty past four on Thursday finds the form in a wallet, sees RETURN BY [DATE] — THIS FORM IS A PUBLIC DOCUMENT ONCE COMPLETED, and fills in two boxes in biro in the correct order: `106` in number of occupied addresses, and `M. OYELARAN — CHIEF OFFICER — CARROW LANE HA — BY POST AND TELEPHONE` and the ring number in the second box.** She puts the wallet back and goes home and does not think about it for a day and a half. **On Friday at 09:20 the borough rings: the figure is recorded, it is a public document, and the rent-roll figure for the same four blocks is 122, and the difference of sixteen has to be accounted for.** The answer, in full, and she gets there first: 128 rooms, 122 on a rent roll because six never signed and have never been collected from and have never been chased, 106 occupied because twenty-two households have moved out, and **the only difference the two public numbers show anybody is sixteen — sixteen rooms on a rent roll with nobody in them, which is a void, which is a landlord's exposure figure, and which reads as bad management, and her own sentence is *the legible difference is sixteen and sixteen is void and void is my fault*. The number the association is actually paid for is ninety-two, because Block C has been on a void allowance since 6 May, and the shortfall is £4,361.60, and that is on no form.** **And then the half she actually wanted said, which she asked to have written down: 106 minus 92 is fourteen, and eight of those fourteen are the eight households still in Block C who have been carrying the water for thirty rooms since 1998, and six are the six who never signed — and those two numbers are the only ones anybody in this borough would recognise as hardship and the only two that cannot be on anything, because every document that could hold them is a list of who does not pay.** The sixteen is sixteen empty rooms on a rent book and she could have ended ten of them in April if she had had four hundred pounds. She will not give the other fourteen and will not produce a list, and has refused three times this week. **The afternoon she had left was the afternoon**, and eleven households in a hostel had asked for the block meeting and the woman in the hostel whose telephone does not work rang at a quarter past ten. **And the ending, which is a decision and not an event: on Friday at eleven o'clock Royden Achebe has to file nine hundred words on ground he cannot reach, and Jonas Mercer will be in a lowland chamber because Emeka Rusk rang at ten past eight and said the screens were worse. He goes. He does not ask. He does not say that he is going and he does not say that he will not be out before half past twelve. That is the arrangement, and it was made on the Wednesday afternoon in about nine words on a telephone, and neither of them said out loud at the time that he was arranging it and neither of them says so now. The filing is in Chapter 77.**

### Chapter 0077 — The Report on 6-L/14

**Thursday 29 May into Friday 30 May. A lowland chamber, a works van doorway on the tide, a records office at ten past one.** **The report is filed at 11:00 in a room in Gantry Ward with two doors and nobody from this account in it.** Jonas is at the bottom of a lowland chamber with a torch and does not try to reach the ground, and Pell's standing rule from 18 April holds: a gallery is for people who want to listen, and if anybody puts a microphone on him the registrar will stand up and say he is not a party. **Eleven pages, nine hundred words, correct: the chamber is not maintainable, the bank is not maintainable, the works owed are four tonnes of stone and one sill beam, about £1,180, in one line without argument. And the burden should be released. And the last page, in four lines: a burden is not a claim; a burden cannot be refused by the person who holds it; a burden may be released only as a claim, and a claim only to a successor who consents; the successors are the persons who would use the structure; there is no party that answers for 6-L/14 and there has not been one since 6 April; he is not permitted to name a successor; he has looked for a way to name a street for eight weeks and there is not one and he has concluded the reason there is not one is the correct reason.** **And the report contains no resident of the Weir and no person of any kind, and getting it to that state took three decisions in three rooms in eight weeks: Pell's registrar's record of 18 April, which read a condition line into a public record and put under it, for the file and not for any person, that the condition line on 6-L/14 was available to be recorded on 1 April and was not; Achebe taking the interim post in the Assembly on 1 April at two o'clock, standing up, after Jonas declined it in the room, on the ground that a department cannot hold a plate and a man can — and then, on 18 April, a registrar attaching a piece of ground he cannot reach to that post, which he accepted by telephone at about 19:06 out of a yard in Gantry Ward in nine words, saying he would rather have had it before he had the post, which he has now said twice; and six words written in eleven minutes in a records room on 14 March by a probationary grade three technician — `NON-CIVIC PLANT` — accepted at the Reclamation Season review so that no civic claim attaches to the chamber or the bank. Nobody has asked him to unwrite them and nobody in this city knows they are his except the two men. And no civic claim means no invoicee, so the £1,180 of real work cannot go on anybody's schedule. He has known since 2 April that the six words are the probationary technician's and has not asked him about it.** **The report names a duty held in a different instrument by the same man: page nine, paragraph three, the outfall inspection duty at 6-L/16, transferred 17 May, is not discharged by this report. That duty is in Nina Calder's typing in another building; this report is on the field services file in a drawer in a cabinet in a corridor in Gantry Ward. Neither file knows the other exists and there is no rule anywhere that anybody has to read both.** The acceptance will be a level-three tray, eleven weeks, a signature. **What that makes is not a vacancy, it is a line: a line on a file with his name in it and `reported not maintainable, release recommended, not released` in the next column. A line like that does not sit on a file for a year. Lines like that get found.** **And Nina Calder's sentence, on Friday afternoon, unasked, once, in advance:** the last time there was a gap in that file it was 2011, it got filled inside about four months by a man sent out from estates, the thing that got him the job was being told the line was plant, and from about six things said near her in nine years without anybody meaning to say them, he has a fortnight somewhere in this city with a bucket under a tap. That is all of it. He is not a bad man. She has worked with him for nine years and has never been able to ask him. She asked for nothing, said the arrangement with Jonas has been in place since April, and would not see the book.

### Chapter 0078 — What a Heat Protocol Asks A Landlord For

**Friday 30 May into Saturday 31 May. A corridor at Saint Orra west at ten past eight, a Methodist hall at ten past six and ten past ten.** **THE LETTER AND THE CONVERSATION ARE NOW ON THE PAGE, WHICH CHAPTERS 60–70 DID NOT CONTAIN: Gunnar Halvorsen tells Dr Sanaa Iqbal at 08:10 on Friday 30 May, on foot, in daylight, in front of about thirty people, because that is what he said he would do on 9 May.** He says the truth first: the letter went on his desk at 09:00 on Monday the nineteenth, he did not tell her on the nineteenth, and he has had eleven days to work out why, and the reason is that on the nineteenth he was about to be in a Committee room. He gives her the A4 and says he is not asking her for anything, and that he knows two things about her and is not pretending to know only one: that she would have said no and been right, and that she would have said yes and been right and would not have enjoyed it, and it would have gone into a governance file with a paragraph number in eleven weeks. **She says thank you for telling me, and then in the next breath: I am not thanking you, those are the words, and I am not saying it was right and I am not saying it was wrong — and the rule in this building that stops anybody asking anybody about a date is a good rule and a correct rule and it means nobody ever tells anybody anything, and the people it protects are the four people and not the twenty thousand. And: I am not going to use you, or a letter, or a budget line, or a man with a register, on Monday, and you will not be in that room either, and I have thought about that a great deal and have not told you. He says: I would not have come. She says: that is why I have been able to sleep.** **In the hall on Friday night there are about sixty people and eleven more outside in the road. On Saturday at half past ten there are about forty, and six of the eight Block C households are there, on foot, from four hundred yards, in thirty-one degrees, with their buckets, because there is nowhere else to put them. Jonas Mercer gets there at ten past six and carries chairs in with the caretaker's wife and with DELE FASHANU of Block B, who says the last three men who came into that hall worked for somebody, one had a list, one had a hat, one had a photograph, and you have not got a list and you have not got a hat and I can see you have not got a photograph, and I do not know why you are here and I have decided that I do not mind.** **She is nine minutes late because a woman in the hostel rang. Then: in January I put a standing order form for two pounds sixty a room a week through a hundred and twenty-two letterboxes on a Saturday morning, on foot, with a bag, and I knocked on every door, and I have never once pretended that was a consultation. Then, once, in about four seconds: THE FEE DOES NOT STOP.** And the two of it: two pounds sixty is not a contribution, it is the whole of what keeps a door open in four buildings and puts a man in Block D on a Wednesday for a lift that has not worked since 2011, and the sixteen thousand eight hundred went on a schedule the association did not write, and the four thousand three hundred and sixty-one pounds and sixty pence comes out of her own salary and will go on coming after she is gone, because there is no mechanism by which it stops. **And then: twelve households have asked to leave the agreement, eleven of them in one morning in their own handwriting on their own doorways, and that is a number and not a group, and I am not going to stand here and tell you that twelve households of this road have a view, because they do not, and I am not going to find out in a room. She will not say which ones. She has refused three times this week. And she said on the Friday that she was not going to tell them that clause 4(c) exists, and she is not going to be able to help them when somebody tells them.** **And at about twenty past eleven she raises £11,280 herself, in front of about forty people, and says the sixth sentence she could not finish: the minute says six lowland water points and does not say where they are, and there is no map in this city that has got them on it, and the Committee could not say, and you cannot say which six and I cannot say which six and a man with a lanyard and a September date could not say which six. And then the other side of it: if nobody can say which six the water points are, then nobody can say whose six they are either, and there are people on this road who have been drinking out of a bucket at half five in the morning for sixteen years and would not come tonight, and they are not in the minute and not on the map, and the chair who read the number out on 19 May did it without asking anybody, and I have not decided yet whether I am going to be able to live with that.** Four people look at the man at the back with a bag at his feet, who has not said a word in an hour and a quarter. He says nine words: he wrote down a number a woman in this hall wrote first, a chair read it out in a room, he did not choose the six, and he has never been able to find out who the six are, and that is the whole of what he has done about it. **A woman of about seventy at the door afterwards: you did not say the word schedule once in an hour and a quarter and the only men who come into this hall are the ones who want something in it. He says he has got nothing to add and would have said so at the beginning if he had thought anybody would let him. She says: nobody is going to let anybody, that is the whole of what happens at these meetings.** And the last paragraph: Priya Ranganathan worked her last funded shift at 22:00 on Saturday 31 May and opened the door herself, in a building with a published gap from eleven to half past one and a refused Thursday and a request that it be left where it was; and on a board in a corridor, in a service manager's handwriting, there were twenty words a week against a steriliser and no name against them.

### Chapter 0079 — A Board With Nine Seats On It

**Sunday 1 June into Monday 2 June. A slipway, a kitchen, a room on the second floor of the civic offices.** **He goes up the path at about eleven on foot, in the sun, with a bottle of water he does not drink, unasked, not a party, with nothing to do, and he did not go for thirteen days because not going was correct.** The 6-L/14 plate at the top of the slip has picked up the batch's fourth and last notice: `WHERE TWO INSTRUMENTS DESCRIBE ONE PIECE OF GROUND AND NEITHER KNOWS THE OTHER EXISTS, BOTH OF THEM ARE TRUE, AND THERE IS NO RULE ANYWHERE THAT ANYBODY HAS TO READ BOTH.` **He tells Margo Doré the whole of the 19 May minute in four minutes, in order, with the source, the hand and the date, and does not put the sheet on the table and does not read it out.** She says: say the number again. Then, in two fixed parts, and the second one is the sentence the batch owes her: **you have not done it again and you have done it worse — you kept the sentence and you used it on my water in a room without me — and I am not angry with you, and I want that said out loud in a kitchen, because you are going to go away from here and do something with it: you did the thing you said you would do, I would have said no, my no would have been eleven words and would not have been in a minute, and in September the sentence on the paper will be mine and it will have been in a room.** She pours two cups, which she has not done since 30 April, and offers him no information about the six and he asks for none, and neither of them says the word *schedule* in that kitchen for the twenty minutes he is in it. **And then, at the sink, at about ten past twelve, she refuses a pump in advance, in her own words, and nobody argues, and no pump is offered and none exists:** a man will come up that path in about two months with a van and a clipboard and a scheme and a photograph of a stand, and he will be correct, and the water will come — and she will say no, because a pump is a thing and a thing can be bought, and the day this street has a pump somebody is going to ask what it is attached to, and the answer will be a paper, and the paper will have one of my neighbours' names in a field on it, because that is how any of you have ever kept a door open since the third of April, and I would like it in the record that I said it on the first of June, in advance, to a man who came here for nothing, and not to a pump. The three positions stand, the ring of pencil has nothing in it, nobody is on the rota, and Bo Trimble said there is no water, was told he is not going to fix it, said right, and went home, and nothing was added to him.** **Monday 2 June, a room booked by a records supervisor on her own time with fourteen returned forms and nowhere to put them: eight on Nina Calder's form, four on the borough's, two on both, and the two on both answer for the same meter room.** Ronan Vasquez, 48, estates, eleven years, brought his own answered form to the table to have it recorded that he answered it in nine minutes on 8 May, and said a form which cannot be answered in nine minutes is asking about something else. **FRANCES TOLLEY, committee clerk of twenty-two years, found SECTION 11 OF THE 2008 CONTINUITY REGULATIONS on Friday in about an hour and a half and copied it out by hand because she did not trust herself not to improve it. It is permissive — may be constituted. A body of that kind has NINE SEATS: one for each service district drawn by the layer, and a NINTH, for a person who answers for a service and is not a body, not a veto, not a chair, cannot be removed by the other eight. It is called A COOPERATIVE STEWARD BOARD. She did not put it to the chair and said so, and gave the whole of her reason: a return with nowhere to go is a return that has not been made, and I have known that for twenty-two years and I have never had to write it down before.** **OREN PELL SAYS NO, at a volume that makes a woman two floors down look out of a door, and says he is not the man who killed it, and this is the best thing he says in the volume:** a body of nine seats has one power — it may require a party holding continuity to answer, receive the answer, and be asked to consider a matter — and it cannot require a person to do anything, hold a duty, or answer a question, and there is no appeal from it to anybody, and the reason there is no appeal is page nine. **So the ninth seat is a chair. Not a chair with a person in it, which is what a person always is. A chair with a person in it that nobody can ask a question. A steward board whose ninth seat cannot be challenged is not a steward board. It is a register with chairs. And every one of the four hundred and sixty pages of that schedule is a register with chairs.** He asked for his job title to be written next to it. Frances Tolley said: he is right. The chair, from a desk in the Old Ward: **she will not constitute a body at all on the strength of a permissive power found in a corridor on a Friday afternoon, and will say so in writing if anybody puts it in writing.** Pell: then write it down, that is the second useful thing anybody has done in this room in nine days. **And at 11:20 the aide rings back with a date: THE NEXT SITTING IS THURSDAY 19 JUNE 2014, TEN IN THE MORNING, COMMITTEE ROOM TWO; the returns are to be read into the record as they are received; the definition of *holder* is on the papers if and when a definition is before the Committee and the chair will not arrange one.** Frances Tolley writes the two facts on the same piece of paper, one above the other, with the date on both, in eleven seconds, and says that is not a finding and in about four years somebody will work out that the state of the world in this city is determined by whether anybody rings anybody. **Nina Calder found a fourth list on Friday and stopped before writing anything on it: a register of attachment with one name, a register of narrowings with fourteen on a public board, and a proposed board with nine seats. A list is not a record. A record is about one thing at one time. A list is a set of things somebody intends to go on adding to, and the intention is the danger.** And the ending, to a table with two people in it: **for nine seats to be anything other than nine chairs, one of the nine has to be able to be put in a room and told to stop, and removed in front of the other eight, by people who did not put him there. That is the whole of it. There is no second half.** She is not asking a question, is not expecting an answer, and asks anybody who disagrees to say so in writing before 19 June. **The board was not formed. Nothing was constituted. Nobody has been charged with making it exist.**

### Chapter 0080 — The June Reckoning

**Sunday 8 June into Monday 9 June, ten in the morning. A yard, then level two at Saint Orra west.** Sunday: four things, none of them preparation — up at half past six, forty minutes' walk, a bracket on the shop's riser cabinet done well in eleven minutes, and fifty minutes on a crate with the pad in his pocket not taken out. **Bea Osunde gives him one of four sentences and keeps three for Wednesday: she has told him not to bring anything and the reason is not manners, and if he turns up with a sheet he has been carrying since April then whatever she says in that room will be about the paper and he will have spent the only thing he has ever been good at in this city in order to be the second man in the room holding one.** He brings nothing. **The review of the west clinic's own witness structure, nine people, four of them on that roster: Ruth Nwosu, Ezra Mbeki, Bram Osei, Priya Ranganathan, plus Gunnar Halvorsen, Mina Okafor, Yvonne Amoah (ambulance duty officer, eleven years) and Fiona Grieve (52, trust governance and quality, who said she had no standing and spoke three sentences all morning). Jonas sits at the back on a chair from the corridor with nothing in his hands and is given one question in advance.** **She does not open with a finding. She opens by putting the rota board face up — the third of June, not written on since 9 May — and says she is not going to defend it.** The finding: **the renewal clause has not failed because it was badly written. It has failed because a rota does not exist.** And then the four sentences she has thought about since 4 April, in the order she has them: the clause needs two people in a corridor at ten at night; it does not say which two; it cannot, because naming a person in a clause makes a person a holder, and a holder may be asked what it will do and not what it will cost. So the clause depends entirely on a rota, and a rota is not a clause — a rota is a thing four tired people make for themselves on a Friday out of nothing with no money in nine minutes, and it is the only instrument in this city that has ever actually opened a door, and there has not been one in this building since 12 February. **A clause that depends on a rota is a clause that will break on the first surge, and the second time it breaks the gap is longer, and the third time somebody will write a schedule, and the schedule will be a good schedule — clear, priced, with a term and a renewal and a party who answers, and it will cost somebody £4,200 and keep the door open — and the reason it will be adopted is not that anybody is wicked, it is that by the third time everyone in the room will be so tired that a thing with a party who answers will sound like a mercy. And I want that in the minute before I am asked to chair anything else.** Nobody contradicts her. **And then the other half, saved for last, because nobody in this city will say it out loud for a year: **The forty hours are the failure, and she says so first, in those words.** 06:00 Tuesday the fifteenth of April to 22:00 Wednesday the sixteenth, 2014: a woman of eighty-four in bay six for the whole of it, a man of eighty-one who wanted his own sheets, about forty other people, and she signed the clause. **And eight days later, on the night of Wednesday the twenty-third of April, the same structure did the other thing.** Not despite. Because of.. Not despite. Because of.** It was not for anybody in that room. It was a night orderly of twenty-nine who was not on anybody's rota writing four words in nine seconds in biro on the back of a laundry delivery note so that a mortuary gate would not narrow to its own maintenance, and a registrar of sixty-one declining to strike them in ninety seconds for the correct reason, which was that striking them empties the field and the maintenance on a mortuary gate is a man with a key. **A witness arrangement is a promise made by people who are tired at ten at night. That is the only kind of promise anybody in this city has ever been able to make. If anybody proposes replacing this with something that is not made by tired people at ten at night, they need to read April first, and not the finding and not the recommendation. The forty hours. That is what it was for.** **And the deepest refusal in the batch is hers, and it is the finding.** The steriliser on level zero runs on £410 and a logged weekly verification of twenty minutes a week. The supplier's regime is signed by her. The supplier will take a signature from any named member of staff. **Since 9 May the log has carried no name and no signature and the verification has not happened once.** If it does not happen before the quarterly visit in July there is a file with no signature in it and a compliance paragraph in the autumn, with a paragraph number, in front of four hundred and twenty women. **She could do it and did not and said so: if her name is against twenty minutes a week, then twenty minutes a week has got a person on it, that person is a doctor, and she is the one already in the building at eleven at night and seven in the morning, and then it is a rota with one name on it, and a rota with one name on it is what everybody in this room has been refusing for four months, and Bram's Thursday is a rota with one name on it and he asked for it to be left in exactly those words. And I could do it. And I am not going to do it, and the reason I am not going to do it is the same reason this structure has no rota, and neither of us is being cowardly, and both of those refusals are the failure.** **Yvonne Amoah, minuted: that is the first time in eleven years I have heard a doctor say I could do it and then not do it. Mina Okafor wrote it down.** **Bram Osei, having said nothing for eighty minutes: he has refused that Thursday since the first week of March because he could not do it and could not say why, it is a bad refusal, he would rather be the bad one on the wall than part of a good one on a form, and the second refusal anybody puts on that board will be asked about it.** **Priya Ranganathan: her name came off the funded line on Friday, and she would like it to stay on the record of who has held it, because in about two years somebody is going to ask whether the second clinician ever existed, and she did, and she was on it for eight weeks, and she was paid out of a coffee-and-cakes appeal from 2011, and she would like that in a document while there is still one person left who remembers the appeal. It goes in, unedited, with Bram's, both unedited.** **Jonas's one question: the decontamination log. Was there a name on it. No. Was there a name on any of them. No — not one signature since 9 May, no facility on the log for a name, the machine logs its own chamber temperature on a strip that goes in a drawer, and the weekly verification is a person looking at the strip and writing their name under it. At the quarterly visit in July the supplier will ask for the log, and if there is no log there is a non-conformance, and a non-conformance has a paragraph number, and a paragraph number is on a public compliance report in the autumn, in front of four hundred and twenty women.** **What she asked for is a record and not a rota**: a document with a date saying what the five lines are and when they are read and by whom and what a person may refuse and what happens when a person refuses, and saying in its own fourth line that it is not a service, that it is unpaid, and that any proposal to make it a service must come back to that table. Fiona Grieve's three sentences: it goes on the governance file today, it is not a compliance document and must not be produced as one, and the fourth line is to be copied word for word into anything that describes this clause anywhere else in this city, without amendment and without a summary. And one more thing, and she does not soften it: **we have a practice, not a rota, and a practice is a real thing and it is not nothing, and I will not have it called a rota in a minute, because the day it is called a rota somebody will be asked to sign it. And I would rather somebody write a schedule on the first surge than make a promise at ten at night. I have read all four hundred and sixty pages. Page nine is a good sentence. It is the sentence that has made a person never in the way, and I have built a career out of a service and I am telling nine people on a Monday morning that I would rather have the practice than the service, because the practice can be refused and the service cannot.** The terms are still six days, the gate still runs on its local term, the 11:00–13:30 gap is still published and unfilled, the refused Thursday is still on the board, and **he renewed nothing and brought nothing.** Then four lines in the pad, and they are the last four lines he wrote in the first week of June, and nobody read them until the summer.

## Rolling Handoff 8 — VOLUME 02, AFTER THE HEAT

**The heat happened and it did what weather does, and it was measured four miles away on the saltmarsh at one station, and there is no instrument in this city that will tell you what the temperature is on a street.** A public-health notice activated on 26 May and asked every residential care premise to confirm occupancy and a named responsible person for every address in its custody. **The Carrow Lane Housing Association is inside that definition because of a gas inspection in 2011 and not because of care, and the answer went in without the chief officer's knowledge or authority, in biro, on 28 May at twenty past four in the afternoon, by a clerk of sixty-one who filled in two boxes in the correct order: 106, and her name and the ring number.** 128 rooms, 122 on the rent roll, 106 occupied, 92 rooms actually paid for. **The only difference anybody can see is sixteen empty rooms on a rent roll, which is a void and reads as bad management and is her own fault; 106 minus 92 is fourteen, and eight of those are the eight Block C households carrying water in buckets since 1998 and six are the six who never signed, and those are the only numbers anybody would recognise as hardship and the only ones that cannot be on anything, because every document that could hold them is a list of who does not pay. **She will not produce a list. The answer to any request for a list of the people in her stock is two rooms above a betting shop on Carrow Lane and that line is in her own file from 2016.**

**Block C has been without water since Tuesday 6 May 2014 and its node is still on Tier 2 and £4,200 a year is being paid for the continuity of a node with no water behind it.** The stopcock has never had a lock and the tag says 1998 and the freeholder's agent wants four meters out of the cupboard. Twenty-two households are in a hostel and two flats at Oyelaran's own expense. Eight households have been carrying the water for thirty rooms in buckets since 1998 and it has never been in a letter, and the one letter they have asked for twice in sixteen years is the cheapest thing in this account and nobody has written it. **And the eight who carry the water are the only people in that block with a reason to want the schedule to keep working, and they are not on it, and they cannot be.**

**A service manager with £2,140 in an activity budget spent £1,880 of it in daylight, at a table, on a form, at ten in the morning on Tuesday 26 May, to put a tank feed on Tier 1, and he said twice that it was the only door in the room, and he was right, and everybody in the building agreed he was right, and Jonas Mercer's part was to do nothing, correctly, and then to sit with why the doing-nothing was the whole of what he had.** £260 is left. The 11:00–13:30 gap is still a gap. A continuity fee buys a node and is not an hour of anybody's time, and he said that out loud in advance, unasked, and said that if anybody in that building ever tells a member of staff this fee has paid for a gap in a rota then they are lying and he will deny having heard it.

**Eleven Thursday handovers from 6 March to 15 May were performed by a day porter who is not on the roster, unpaid, and Tomas Renn counted them, on purpose, out loud, in a corridor, in front of four people, because the registrar said in April that there are about nine hundred of these and nobody counts them.** Every one of the four gave a correct answer about what happens if two of them say no at the 22:00 handover on 11 October, and none of them answered, because they cannot answer without being on it. Ezra Mbeki asked him not to write it down and he wrote it down anyway and has sent it to nobody. **Priya Ranganathan asked for a Sunday in October to go in front of the board in the same box as Bram Osei's refused Thursday, in the same words, with her name and a date, and Bram said she may not. Nothing has been put in front of the board.** The outfall duty has not moved and cannot be shared, and its record has been made twice, and **Jonas Mercer's name is on that file a second time, at a records supervisor's insistence, for a duty he refused to move.**

**Royden Achebe's report was correct: the chamber and the bank are not maintainable, four tonnes of stone and a sill beam are owed, about £1,180, and the burden should be released and it cannot be, because a burden cannot be refused and a claim can only be released to a successor who consents and the successors are a street with no address.** The report has no resident of the Weir and no person in it of any kind, and there is no civic claim on that ground, so the £1,180 of real work has nobody to be invoiced. **The report names a duty held in a different instrument by the same man, on a different file, in a different building, and no rule anywhere requires anybody to read both.** The acceptance will be a signature in about eleven weeks, and what it makes is not a vacancy but a line that does not sit on a file for a year, because lines like that get found. **And in August somebody will ring Jonas about a Tuesday and the man who will ring him is Royden Achebe, and Jonas has not yet worked out whether he is going to be the one who answers.**

**Margo Doré was told on Sunday 1 June, on foot, at about eleven, that a Tier 1 price on six lowland water points is in a Committee minute, and one of the six is the stand at the top of the slip, and nobody asked her.** She said: you have not done it again and you have done it worse. And: I am not angry with you, because you did the thing you said you would do, and my no would have been eleven words and would not have been in a minute, and in September the sentence on the paper will be mine and it will have been in a room. **She refused a pump in advance, in her own words, to a man who came for nothing and not to a pump, and nobody argued, and no pump exists and none was offered.** The three positions stand, the ring of pencil has nothing in it, nobody is on the rota, and Bo Trimble is findable and nothing may be added to him.

**The determination with a deadline and no date on it was not a deadline, for eight days, and then it had one: the next sitting is Thursday 19 June 2014 at ten in the morning, because a clerk of twenty-two years telephoned an office at eleven o'clock and asked a question she has been allowed to ask since 1992, and wrote the two facts on the same piece of paper, one above the other, with the date on both.** A cooperative steward board — nine seats, one per service district the drawing layer draws, and a ninth for a person who answers for a service and is not a body — was named in a Committee transcript in May and named again on a table on 2 June, and refused twice. **Pell's reason is page nine, and it is the best thing he says in the volume: a steward board whose ninth seat cannot be challenged is not a steward board, it is a register with chairs, and every one of the four hundred and sixty pages of that schedule is a register with chairs. Nothing was formed. Nobody has been charged with making it exist. And the condition has been stated and not answered: for nine seats to be anything other than nine chairs, one of the nine has to be able to be put in a room and told to stop, and removed in front of the other eight, by people who did not put him there.**

**And on Monday 9 June at ten in the morning, in a room with nine people in it, the doctor who signed the clause in the first week of April said the thing the whole of this volume has been walking towards: the renewal clause has not failed through drafting, it has failed through the absence of a rota; a practice of unpaid, unscheduled, voluntary witnessing held a care entrance open and is not a service and cannot be made into one by any instrument held by the borough, the contractor, or any person; the third time a surge breaks this, a schedule will be written, it will be a good schedule, and it will be adopted. And she would rather have the practice than the service, because the practice can be refused and the service cannot — and she refused to put her own name against twenty minutes a week on a decontamination log, and said she could do it, and gave the reason, and the reason is the finding, and the log has carried no signature since 9 May and the quarterly visit is in July and four hundred and twenty women in this borough have their instruments out of that room every weekday.**

**The claims box closes Friday 18 July 2014. The next sitting is Thursday 19 June. The next quarterly report is September. The schedule expires 06:00 Sunday 12 October 2014 and the crossing is the 22:00 handover on Saturday the eleventh, and nothing has happened about it since 22 May.**

---

## Volume 02, Batch 0004 — Chapters 81–90

**81 — The Nineteenth of June.** The ten days after Monday 9 June are other people's: a shop, a pad with nothing on it, the corridor board, the decontamination log with fifty-two empty signature boxes, and four telephones he does not make. **Thursday 19 June, ten o'clock, committee room two: a determinations meeting on fourteen returns in which every single thing said is correct.** A chair reads into the record two answers about METER ROOM 3, SALTER'S YARD that cannot both be the answer, and asks for it recorded that the instruction to read as received is the reason there are two. **Jonas's one question, in nine words: is anybody going to telephone the person in that field. The chair answers no, on page nine, and asks for it to be minuted three times, and says if she has not been told then somebody will tell her and it will not be this Committee.** Pell: *the records office is required to.* **Frances Tolley finds the chain at ten past one by putting two pieces of paper next to each other**, and Pell names the parent, and states the method — a price says what a thing costs, a chain says who is standing behind the price, and the only door is to put the last person in the chain somewhere somebody has to ask her a question. **Rook answers, and is right about all of it, including the ninety-day notice.** In the corridor Pell will not say the name: she is findable since 7 May, a person who has not been asked may not be named, and only the party who holds the claim can withdraw it.

**82 — A Trust With A Folder.** Friday 20 June: the assignment clause at page 219 — *the assignee takes the claim, and takes what is owed on the claim, and takes the duty of the claim* — and a walk out to Kestrel Hill to a gatepost. Monday 23 June, a room above a letting agency: **Beverley Annis, Dorothy Cresswell, Dorothy Alderman, Ceri Vaughan and Alden Rook**, four documents laid out in date order with a centimetre between them — one placing and three assignments — and one question asked. **The last link is not an assignment and nobody signed it: on 3 June a managing agent read the plate in a meter cupboard under a stair — *the holder of this point pays for it and the point is not the payer's* — and entered the named steward's name in the holder field, correctly, and had never spoken to her.** Ceri Vaughan, who made the 2 May assignment and does not want to be asked, answers it correctly anyway: a share of a claim carries the tier and not the work, so a duty got lighter three times and no one was ever given the job — and **nobody in the chain has ever asked what happens when the person at the end says no, because a person cannot be challenged.** Rook: a share of a fee is cheaper than a queue, and if you break this chain you do not fix that, you just leave her standing at the bottom of nothing. **Monday afternoon: a woman telephones the records counter, is told the truth because the counter is required to, writes the reference on the back of her hand, and asks whether anybody is going to come and tell her what it is.**

**83 — What Thirty-One People Signed.** Tuesday 24 June, the records office: thirteen of the fourteen returns were answered by the party holding the node; the fourteenth was answered on 16 June by a maintenance firm on behalf of a person it had never spoken to, and a person in a field is not a party until somebody tells her. Thursday 26 June, the monthly block meeting, half past seven, twenty-six of thirty-one. **Clause nine comes out of a coat and is read aloud, with a photograph of the meter in it, and thirty-one people see it for the first time in fourteen years.** The fee rise is discovered wrongly and Fofana lets it stand — *the block's water went onto a paid schedule on the eleventh of March* — and that is the first time in nineteen years she has managed a room by leaving something out. **The turn: the twelve did not refuse a price, they refused a document.** The four who missed a month are not listed and the twelve are not asked to sign. **And Esio Kotto, 29, works out in nine seconds that nineteen and twelve are both going to be legible to anybody who walks into a counter in this city**, and the block meeting is called for 2–4 July, and the steward decides to do it in her own words, in her own room, and to ask nobody to sign anything.

**84 — The Node With a Person In It.** Friday 27 June, twenty-seven minutes past two, outside Wren Court. **How long have you known.** *That is the first correct thing anybody from that building has done in nineteen years.* She will not answer on a step, and she will not answer on one person's word ever again: she will say it twice, and the second time in front of thirty-one people. He asks his one question — *what will you do* — and she answers, and then she asks **who pays, when a resident refuses the price**, in a stairwell, in nine words, and he cannot answer, and she does not need him to, because she has already worked out that the somebody who is not them is her, unpaid, findable, unchallengeable, and good until she is sixty-one, and that **there is no version of this where thirty-one people hold a node.** Monday 30 June, the workshop, six people: four documents read out in her own words, and **her own correction of the count — *three assignments, not four, because one of the four is a placing and a placing is not a hand*** — and the arithmetic — **£72 a month of somebody else's money on the block's water bill, four charges since 11 March, £2,431.16 paid out of a workshop and never mentioned.** And three things she requires before she will instruct it, **one of which is that he does not do it.**

**85 — The Mending He Does Not Make.** Monday 30 June, 09:20, Pell rules that the standing must be given twice and that the second time is the one that is entered, and that the entry is Thursday 3 July at nine, and the term runs to six on Friday. **Tuesday 1 July, 06:40, a stand at the bottom of Quarry Street, nine households, a woman of thirty-four with a trolley going to a shift that starts at seven, and he does not ask her anything.** 11:00, the meter cupboard under the stairs at Wren Court, tool roll unopened, four lines of her handwriting on the shelf, and **he says it out loud to nobody and refuses**, in his own words, at length: the man who found a chain is not the man who ends it, because she cannot be on the plate, and a record with one name in it cannot be argued with. Wednesday 2 July, 09:04, Pell reads the four lines and **enters nothing**, and says come back Thursday. 23:00, the workshop, twenty-nine people, and the second time, said aloud, to a room: *I am instructing that the claim at this node be taken off, and I am not authorising anybody to put anything in its place.* He is at the back and is not asked to speak and says nothing, which is correct.

**86 — The Fee Does Not Stop.** Thursday 3 July, 09:00, the entry is made and the second statement goes into a registrar's record in her own words, unedited. That evening, and Thursday and Friday, the block sits. **The money is the part that could be checked:** a charge of seventy-two pounds comes out of the workshop account at ten past six on Friday 4 July and the block's books man rings at a quarter past seven to say one sentence about it, and the same seventy-two is credited back within the hour. **The fee does not stop and is thirteen pounds sixty and nothing is signed and there is no minute.** Clause nine is read out in full four times and not tidied. The twelve are not asked to sign and the four are not written down. The riser is nine hundred pounds, the tin turns over about thirteen thousand four hundred and thirty-six pounds a year, and the seventy-two that stopped is going back to a trust in Kestrel Hill, and **the riser is two years away, and nobody can raise it without producing a list of who can.** And at eleven on the Friday night, in four sentences, thirty-one people who are not a body arrive at the third condition: **one person, findable, holding a thing she cannot be asked about by any of the thirty-one whose water it is.** In the doorway, Esio Kotto produces the only date anybody has — the fourth of August — and nobody in the room knows whose Thursday it is. Friday afternoon, the shop, and Bea Osunde: *do not write it down, because whatever it is, it has got somebody else's name on it and not yours, and that is the good version.*

**87 — What The Revocation Cost.** Friday afternoon in the yard, Bea Osunde: *you have got a face on this week like a man who has won something.* A four-page attachment in the flat register, and Jonas puts it on a Return of a Deferred Node as an attachment because that is the honest use of an attachment field. **Nine items: it is correct; one person at the bottom of Quarry Street lost one day and seven hours — 11:00 Wednesday 2 July, the second standing, to 06:00 Friday 4 July — out of a total delay of three days and twenty hours of which one day and twenty-three hours is his, and she is not named and he has never had her name; £2,431.16 saved; a riser; KH-4/20 left at Tier 2 to 4 August with eleven patients above it; a minute that cannot be corrected; a return due again on 4 August from a body whose first answer cost a clerk of sixty-one an afternoon; a notice with a date; and one name, MERCER, J.** Monday, the counter, and **Nina Calder reads all nine items out in a public room in her flat voice and a woman waiting about a tenancy reads it over her shoulder.** And then: **the fourth page.** She had put it in the bin tray on Friday at twenty past four and taken it out on Monday at half past eight, and it is not a list — one week, one place, one number, what was counted, and one sentence saying what it is not, and **a person may refuse to be counted and the count then says so and says how many refused.** Pell telephones at four on the Monday, having had the annex-six clause since the Thursday the notice went out at eleven, and on and after 1 October the placing party may re-assert the claim, at the tier, at that node, **without the consent of any person whose name appears in the holder field of that node at any time before that date.**

**88 — A Registrar's Answer.** Tuesday: Calder says no twice to the same question; Rook says don't ask, you will only be a man who was told no, and you have got the date. Tuesday afternoon, the shop, and **Tomas Renn brings the question nobody has asked: what happens on the day after the first of October, when she is in the field again because she has to be, and a consent can be given twice — and that is not defeating the clause, it is complying with it, twice.** And he will not talk about the eleventh of October in a shop, and neither will Jonas, and that is now the rule between them. Wednesday 9 July, ten o'clock: **a chain can be broken by the last holder and by nobody else, eleven seconds; there is no mechanism to make the top of a chain pay and none to make a person take a duty, and those are the same sentence; and the fourth ruling of the year is that a claim discharged for the holder is not discharged for the person who caused it, and that findability is not answerability, and that nothing in it exonerates anybody.** Pell is tired since 11 June and says so. In the corridor, nine words, and then: **there is a regulation about this, it is 2008, it is section eleven, and Frances Tolley has a copy of it in her own hand, and it has been read in this office twice and neither time by anybody who could act on it.**

**89 — Two Days In September.** Thursday 10 July, a room above a building society, and Gareth Raynor-Hale's September report being written in July with a section headed *lowland evacuation capacity: unresolved* and three weeks to the printer. **Nine hundred and forty is not nine hundred and forty people, it is nine hundred and forty times that somebody stood at the barrier end and could not get across, and it is one woman's own private record and she has never worked out the number of people because then it would be a number of people.** Jonas tells him about Dev Singh's empty-crossing clause a second time, and asks for his own silence to go in Raynor-Hale's words rather than his, and Raynor-Hale refuses and writes it in his own, which is worse for Dev and better for the report. **And he refuses the number he could have manufactured in four minutes — six — and says the sentence instead, and says a report that can be matched to a list of addresses is two documents away from a document with people on it.** The forum's eleventh place is empty and is not written *vacant* and Raynor-Hale wants the reason minuted by somebody who is not him. Friday 11 July, twenty-five past four: **a woman telephones the shop, gives no name, says she is one of the eight, and says the letter has been asked for twice and nobody has written it, and says there is no date on it and that is why she is telephoning a shop.** He does not ring the number back.

**90 — The Floor.** Monday 14 July, the corridor at Saint Orra west: the 11:00–13:30 gap still published and unfilled, Bram Osei's refused Thursday still on the board in his own words with *no reason I can give* under it, Priya Ranganathan's card still there at her own insistence, terms still six days, and the decontamination log with no name against any of fifty-two Sundays. Tuesday, the Old Ward board room, **eleven feet by nine**: the register of narrowings with **fifteen** entries — fourteen on 1 June plus the Salter's Yard node that came off a schedule on 4 July — and a blank line, the claims box with its ninety-day card, the laminated heat protocol nobody has read aloud, and the occupancy condition restated in truncated form. Wednesday 16 July, 22:10, a stairwell: **Ivo Bamba refuses to be thanked — *if you thank me I am a person in a record, and a person in a record is a person somebody can ask, and the name on the schedule would not be mine and it would be the next night orderly.*** And eleven minutes later **Mina Okafor asks whether the practice can be written down, and Yvonne Amoah says no, and says the record exists and is a record and not a rota, and that the fourth line of the 9 June finding says any proposal to make it a service must come back to that table, and that the only nine people who have read the finding are the nine in the room, and that nobody in that building has told the people doing the practice what is in it, and that this is the worst correct thing anybody in this city has done this year.** Thursday 17 July: the supplier comes, the log is on the wall, **a non-conformance is raised and the engineer says anybody in the building can sign it, once a week, twenty minutes, and it is nobody's job, and nobody signs it.** Friday 18 July, ten to five: **the claims box is closed from the outside, the one card about a stair goes into a sealed envelope and a drawer on a lower floor, and the box is empty, and Jonas Mercer stands eight feet away with a folded piece of paper and does not put it in.** Then Gantry Ward at seven, and a chalk board, and a nine-millimetre ghost that reads TIDEGLASS 3 and that nobody rubbed out, and Rook in the doorway saying the board is the best thing in the borough and that it has never been copied in four months not because it is expensive but because it could not be copied without finding somebody to answer for it. And four lines on a pavement: the two dates belong to other people, the September report has a hole in it, and the volume's question restated and not answered.

---

## Volume 02, Batch 0005 — Chapters 91–100 (Section E — The Floor, and the close of the volume)

**91 — The First Of October Is A Wednesday.** Monday 4 August, ten to six, the vestibule. **Two dates arrive at once and neither makes a noise.** The heat-protocol return falls due, and **the number that goes in the ring-number box is NINETY-TWO** — chosen on purpose by a chief officer who has read about the first one; 106 in May, 92 in August, both public, both legible, and the difference between them is a number and not a household, and there is no instrument in this borough that can turn a difference into a household. Calder refuses to explain the reasoning and refuses to be asked twice. **And KH-4/20 lapses at 06:00, and no last verified essential use has ever been recorded, so the residential floor of a private clinic in Kestrel Hill — eleven patients and a day room — becomes a stair, a door and a lift.** The plate says so at ankle height. It keeps working because a man of sixty-one put a fire door wedge on the floor in May, and he has a key that no longer turns the plate, and he says *don't tell me it's nothing's changed, because something has changed*, and asks for nothing. Jonas Mercer tells nobody, is not a party, has no authority over a freeholder's building, and does not ring the trust. **He does not fill in the decontamination log and does not do the twenty minutes, and says why out loud to a bench in an empty room: filling it in is a service, and a service is what the 9 June finding is against, and he is not going to be the man who signs the first line of one.** Tuesday 12 August: the level-three tray for 6-L/14 went to Gantry Ward on a van and **a signature is going to be made by whoever opens it, and you cannot move a signature, and the person is not going to be told what any of the words mean.** Fifty-eight days to 1 October on the Monday; fifty on the Tuesday.

**92 — Somebody Who Is Not A Doctor.** Wednesday 14 August, a telephone: **Royden Achebe rings about a Tuesday, as he has known since 18 May that he would, and he is not asking Jonas Mercer to do the duty — he is asking him to turn up, keep his hands off the plate, write down the times, and have it entered twice. Jonas Mercer is the one who answers.** At the counter, the 6-L/14 acceptance came back signed on Wednesday 13 August by a man whose job is to open trays, and Jonas Mercer is not entitled to a copy. On the corridor at Saint Orra west, Mina Okafor has been holding a note from **Ronan Vasquez, estates, 48, eleven years**: *it is not a service, it is a person reading a strip, and I have held the maintenance of that room's pipework since 2011, and if anybody makes this a rota I will stop and they can have the paragraph number instead.* Thursday 15 August, 16:20: **he signs the acceptance, in a flat sheet in a plastic wallet, having thought about it since Thursday afternoon. His name goes on a log and does not go on the board in the corridor, and both of those are true and neither cancels the other, and he says he will do it until the end of October and that after October it will lapse, because a job in November has a department in it.** **Nina Calder's rider, copied word for word into a compliance document for the first time in this city, carrying the fourth line of the 9 June finding.** Monday 18 August, the supplier: the forward half is granted, **the historical half cannot be reconstructed, and the non-conformance carries forward onto the annual report in the autumn with its paragraph number.** Amoah: *it was not a rota on the Wednesday, it is a paragraph in the autumn, and that is worse, because you can appeal a rota and you cannot appeal a paragraph.* **And Dr Sanaa Iqbal gives him a patient-level fact — bay nine, level one, a node going since 4 July — and asks for nothing, and says the reason out loud: he is the only person in this city who cannot be required to do anything, and that is the only reason he is safe to hand a fact to.**

**93 — The Nineteenth Of June Minute, Again.** Tuesday 19 August, the records office. Frances Tolley brings section 11 out of her own hand — four pages, copied because she did not trust herself not to improve it — and asks for a one-page guidance note. **Nina Calder refuses, for the fourth time, and says the reason in the right order, and then says her condition from 2 June out loud in front of a committee clerk: for nine seats to be anything other than nine chairs, one of the nine has to be able to be put in a room and told to stop, and removed in front of the other eight, by people who did not put him there — and she will not design a form for a body that cannot be asked a question.** Frances Tolley writes it on the back of her own hand. **First time the condition has been minuted by anybody but Calder.** Calder reads all four pages, stops on page four, and tells Tolley to show the whole of it to a records supervisor whether she consents or not. **She reads it on the Tuesday morning. She says she will not tell the ninth seat, and gives the reason — a person may not be required even to be told, and a person who knows she is the only seat that can empty a board is findable in a way she is not now — and nobody argues with her.** A card goes on the Old Ward board, initialled: *a body that cannot be required to do anything cannot be asked what it will do; a body that may not be challenged cannot be told to stop; the two sentences together are the reason a register is not a room.* Thursday 21 August, committee room two: **the Committee again finds it was read to, the two entries stand, and for the first time in four months it does something that is not a form — a reading rule: where a party relies on either entry for Meter Room 3, the other is to be read with it.** The chair answers Jonas Mercer's one question and asks for it minuted: **a counter is required to answer a telephone; a counter is not required to make one; and there is no duty on any officer of this city to tell a person that a thing has happened to them** — and she asks the minute to record how the woman in the field came to know: she rang a counter, and that is the entire chain of causation, and none of it was designed. **The chair's promised record of the nodes with no party answering for them is delivered, one page, a record of shapes, with no name box on it by decision and not by oversight — and it concedes in one line that the spur and the outfall are entry one of the register of narrowings hanging in the ground-floor vestibule of the same building, on the grounds that a register is not a district, an entry is not a holder, and a date on a register is the date it was written down.** In a corridor, Pell: *you have one page of section eleven and it is four, and the answer to your question is on the fourth, and the fourth page is a dissolution clause, and I am not going to read you the fourth page in a corridor, and neither is Nina Calder, and neither is the man with the register.*

**94 — A Public Case.** Monday 25 August, a notice on a board in a shop: **the Carrow Lane Housing Association has applied for a public determination, and Jonas Mercer has still not telephoned her, and she did it herself and told nobody.** He declines to give evidence and says why, and the aide writes it down in the wrong place and he corrects it himself with a biro. Thursday 28 August, committee room two, nineteen in the gallery, a transcript, an unchecked press list. **Marguerite Oyelaran applies against her own interest and does not ask to be relieved of anything.** **Rook does not contest a fact, asks for that to be minuted before she begins, and makes the volume's best argument: we found out; that is the whole of my case and the whole of my weakness; a paid claim is a more trustworthy claim only because the payer found out, and that is a true sentence and a terrible reason to price a door.** She says she does not know what £4,200 bought, that a riser is about £900, that she could have had water in that block by 9 May and did not, and that **£4,200 would not have fixed it.** She describes, in open session and without naming him, six words written in a records room in eleven minutes in March and accepted by a panel on 1 April, and says she has never been able to find out who wrote them and is not going to. **THE RULING, one page, a sub-substitution, limited to precedence: where the order between two nodes is set by the amount of a fee and by no other criterion, the tier so acquired is not a determination of priority — and it does not affect the validity of the Schedule, any placement, the maintenance of any node, the physical works of any party, any fee paid before this date, or the notice served on 3 July; and nothing requires any person to do anything and no fee is recoverable.** The class of node is named: a node whose precedence was bought with a fee and which is not otherwise distinguished by anything that is not itself a fee, **and an empty holder field is the absence of every possible distinction, so a node in a building of thirty-one rented rooms is in the class and there is no consequence provided for.** Jonas Mercer's one question is whether it touches the 3 July notice. It does not. Rook, in a doorway: **a thing that was going to happen anyway now has to be argued for.** A woman in the gallery says the question nobody has answered: the managing agent's meter round is on a Saturday morning at ten o'clock and nobody can say what she will find. **Saturday 30 August is a date held by other people.**

**95 — Nine Seats.** Monday 1 September, a counter: **Nina Calder refuses a one-page standing procedure for the board, for the fourth time, and this time she says why in the right order — the fifth page is the form, the form is a scheme, a scheme is a list with a future in it, and a form is the last thing a person writes and the first thing an institution keeps — and nobody argues with her, and it stands.** Afternoon: a sheet on the Old Ward board. **Eight seats filled by the person named in the answer to a question asked for a state and not for a list, where that person lives under the node they answer for. Nobody was appointed. Nobody was asked in advance. A person named without being asked may decline afterwards, in his own words, and nothing follows from it.** Amoah, Sesay, Fahey, Tarrant, Umeh, Ferris, Crabbe, and — after **one refusal on a piece of paper in a file, by Ronan Vasquez, named on the sheet, dated Friday 22 August, in his own words, and he did not read section 11 and nobody has told him what is in it** — Gloria Nye. **Wren Court, thirty-one rooms, no civic address, was not seated; the room tried; and being right about it is now printed on a board.** Wednesday: Calder tells Jonas Mercer what the ninth seat is, and that she is not going to be told the rest of it. **Thursday 4 September, seven in the evening, the Methodist hall on Carrow Lane, about thirty people: Yolanda Fofana fills the ninth seat in eleven minutes, in an aisle, not on a stage, and states the whole price out loud — one of her, findable since 1995, a lease and a shopfront, never found in nineteen years by anybody holding a document; unpaid, until sixty-one; a person and not a body; not a veto, not a chair, and the other eight cannot remove her; and the thirty-one whose water it is are four hundred yards away and are not seated and she is not going to pretend she is the same thing as them.** She is the one woman out of about forty people there anybody can write to. At ten past nine the board's first act is to have the registrar's statement read out — one power, no way to lose it, nine chairs, the ninth of them is a person — **and a night bus controller of thirty-three puts her hand up and asks *then what are we for*, and they spend an hour and ten minutes on it, and they are not allowed to answer it.**
