**96 — The September Report.** Friday 5 September, a room above a building society, and a proof back since 31 July with a hole in it. **The September quarterly goes to press with a state and a date and a source under the heading *lowland evacuation capacity: unresolved*, and the source is a woman's own private record, in pencil, in a book, kept for nineteen years at the barrier end, and it is nine hundred and forty marks and it is a count of occasions and not of persons.** Raynor-Hale put his own name on it and told Jonas Mercer not to argue, and said it plainly: in about two years somebody is going to go and look at the ledge and the book. **That is the cost, paid on purpose.** A condition goes onto the enamel strip of the Old Ward board, **unsigned and undated and unattributed on its face, and somebody has written two initials and one word on the back of it in pencil and the word is *not mine***, about a consent that may be given twice. Saturday: the barrier end, and the ledge, and a woman of about sixty who is not in the room. **Monday 8 September, twenty to seven, the bottom of Quarry Street: she stops with the trolley and says *you are the one who cost me the day and the seven hours*, and he says yes, and she says she knows it was him because a man came and stood over the plate in the rain and said nothing — and then: *there is no date on me, there is no date on this stand, and I have been keeping to that for eleven years with nobody helping me.* She gives no name. He writes nothing down for two days.** Ten o'clock: the report is tabled. Nora Bell says her sentence twice because nobody wrote it down the first time, and refuses a member's request for a cost per head in the same words, and the chair records that the question was not put in a form that could be answered. Dev Singh is in the room for two hours and Jonas Mercer does not go to him. **Raynor-Hale, in a corridor, is given the question and gives five things and no opinion, and says at the end that he has just had five facts put in front of him with no question on the end of them: the notice was served, the trust has not noticed, the clerk of sixty-three will do her job, and a consent can be given twice and the record of the second is not evidence about the first.** A chief officer of forty-seven has put ninety-two in a public box on purpose and the difference between her two numbers is a number and not a household, and there is no instrument in this city that can turn a difference into a household, and that is the only reason nobody is hurt by it this week and the reason somebody will be hurt by it in four months.

**97 — The Tray.** Monday 15 September, field services, Gantry Ward. **A tray and a person and a date, and Jonas Mercer asks for the index and the officer reads it out to him because it is the only way anybody has ever asked him for anything in two years.** Nine lines, never read in order by anybody: the burden attached 18 April; the eleven pages; the Reclamation Season review of 1 April — *NON-CIVIC PLANT. ACCEPTED. NODE LEAVES THE REVIEW*; **no civic claim, no invoicee**; the duty at 6-L/16 transferred to the holder of this post; the acceptance of Wednesday 13 August — *REPORTED NOT MAINTAINABLE. RELEASE RECOMMENDED. NOT RELEASED*; and a second tray with a query on it. At the counter, **Nina Calder gives him the name: Peter Naylor, forty-four, level three, field services, out of estates since 2011, who has a fortnight somewhere in this city with a bucket under a tap, and who is the reason four tonnes of stone are not going to be bought by anybody, ever, and who does not know that — and the reason she has never been able to ask him is that six words went out of her office in March under a reporting officer number that is not hers, and she typed the form and did not know what they were.** Nobody rings him. **Tuesday 16 September, 06:40, a lowland chamber: Royden Achebe is in Gantry Ward, the duty is his, Jonas Mercer stands on the lip for three hours and eleven minutes and does not go down the ladder, and Emeka Rusk does the work in his own hand with the operator box left blank for the twenty-seventh time since 2 April, and the entry is made twice and nobody is blamed, and Jonas Mercer's name goes in a margin he initialled because there is no field for the man who watched it.** A man in a CivicCore apron comes over the lip and asks for a piece of paper that would let him put four tonnes of stone on a bank tonight. **There is no such paper. Jonas Mercer writes three lines into a margin and gives him a copy, and the third one is the volume's answer to 6-L/14: a man of forty-four, estates, 2011, told a line was plant, got a job out of it, a fortnight with a bucket under a tap, and nobody in this borough has ever been able to ask him one question about it — and that is not a fix.** Pryce: *that is a worse answer than the one I came for.* Wednesday 17 September: **a second tray on a shelf with a rubber band and a date in biro and one question outstanding — is there any party who may consent to the works — and the answer is no and has been no since 29 May.** Jonas Mercer does not answer it and has no standing to.

**98 — The Letter.** Thursday 18 September, the shop. **The first letter he has ever written for anybody, and Bea Osunde finds the only sentence in it that could hurt somebody: *the households of the block* — written three times, once as *the eight households* — and a count in a letter to a managing agent in a year when that block has thirty rooms, no mains water since February 1998 and none at all since the sixth of May, is a number of who.** Calder refuses to type it: a typed letter on this office's machine with a crest at the top is a departmental document, and a departmental document is a record, and a record gets asked for by the next managing agent in four years, and that agent will be looking for a list of who carries water in a block with no water in it. **On the telephone he tells her the price of that: a document is what six agreements in four blocks have not been sent back since January, two of whom have told Oyelaran to her face that they will not sign a thing that makes her name a door, and she has not chased them, and he heard it in a room with a transcript on Thursday.** **Friday 19 September, ten past four, four rings. She does not give her name and he does not ask. He asks one question — what does it have to say — and she says: the year.** *Put nineteen ninety-eight on it and do not put who on it.* She is about seventy-one. **Do not send me a copy and do not tell me that you have sent it: a person who knows that a letter has been sent is a person somebody can ask whether the letter has been answered.** The letter is written in ink, one sheet, no crest, no typed copy, no number eight, no name but one, dated in a sentence. **The wrongness, in the margin of his own draft: the wrongness is not the four meters and it is not the year. The wrongness is that there is his name at the bottom of it and there is nobody else's, and the woman it is for has not asked him to write it, and nobody has asked her anything.** It goes in the post box on Monday 22 September. **Ivy Marsden, seventy-two, is not told and has never been asked, and he has written that down twice.** The block's node is still at Tier 2 at £4,200 and there is no water in that block and the letter is not going to fix a riser.

**99 — What A Person In A Field Is Worth.** Monday 22 September, the shop: **Oren Pell comes to the shop for the first time in his life, on his feet, with a sandwich on the bench, to say that he is finished saying the sentence about who pays for a refusal, and to hand Jonas Mercer a job instead of a ruling — the day you ask somebody, it will not be me and it will not be a committee, it will be a person in a room who can say no, and when they say no that ends the ask.** Then: *do not telephone me about the first of October.* Then, after two minutes: *forty-one minutes, Mr Mercer. I have walked it.* Tuesday: he walks to the bottom of Salter's Yard and does not go up, because the ninth seat is the only person in this city he is allowed to ask, and asking her whenever he wants is a rota, and he has spent five months refusing to be a rota for four other people. **Wednesday 24 September, ten past four, a kitchen off a landing: he asks the question he has carried since 8 March, and she knows what he is carrying before he gets it out, and she refuses to hold it, and she tells him to answer it himself — because he does not owe her an answer, he owes one to the lowland and to a block of 1938 and to the households who carry water, and none of them are in that kitchen. And he answers it, out loud, for the first time in five months: nobody pays, nobody is required, nobody is released — and the only instrument anybody in this city has built in five months that touches any of it is a slip on a public board that says a person may refuse to be counted, and a person who is not counted is not a person who is helped.** Then: **the day after the first of October, she declined to answer it, and she gave the reason, and the reason is that the first person in this city who says that answer out loud is going to be quoted in October by a woman with a lever-arch file who is going to do her job, and the answer is *ask her again tomorrow, she is in the field anyway*, and that is true, and it is the clause working, and it is not defeating anything, it is complying with it twice — and the only thing that stops it is a person who has decided in advance, in her own words, on a date, what she will say the second time. And I am not going to say it out loud to you, because you are a man who writes things down, and I am not going to be that in a drawer. Findability is not answerability.** She tells him not to write it down. He does not write it down. He writes down that he was told not to.

**100 — The Third Time.** Friday 26 September, the vestibule, eleven feet by nine. **The register of narrowings has sixteen entries and has had sixteen since the morning of 4 August, and that is now a stated position: a record of a period, and if it starts again in October then somebody has broken something.** The blank line is blank. The claims box is closed and empty and the envelope is sealed on a lower floor, and on 21 August he went down to that floor and stood in front of the drawer for six minutes and did not open it, and the only reason in the world is that a stair cannot be held in a field, and that is not a good enough reason and it is the reason he has got. **He is asked to chair the board's second sitting and refuses in a corridor with a reason: a chair is a person who decides which of two things gets heard first, section 11 does not require a chair, the ninth seat is not a chair, and in about two years somebody is going to use the word *administrator* about him in a room, and Alden Rook said so in June.** Pell, in a corridor, at last: the other eight cannot remove the ninth, because the board's one power is to require a party to answer and a person is not a party; a registrar can require the board to tell her what she is answerable for, and has, and cannot require her to be removed; **and the fourth page of section 11 is a dissolution clause at six vacancies, and the one seat in that body that cannot be emptied by anybody else is the only seat that can empty it — and he will not say the next sentence, and a clerk minuted that the next sentence was not said, and Calder's reason went under it, and nobody in the corridor added anything.** Saturday and Sunday: he does not go up the Weir path, does not ring the number, does not go to Carrow Lane, and planes the bench, and an old date comes up pale out of the wood. **Monday 29 September, the records counter: a clerk of twenty-two years asks Yolanda Fofana how it feels, and she says it feels like the only one who can get out, and then gives three more minutes, and asks for it to be minuted that this is not modesty.** At ten past five, in the shop, **he writes two dates on the bench in ink along the grain, an inch and a half high: 1 OCT and 11 OCT. Bea Osunde puts the light out over it and says: there is nothing in this borough that can be argued with a mark on a piece of wood that nobody is required to read.** Then Bea, at the bench, says the thing the volume has been refusing to say to him for six months: *the names are the work, Mr Mercer, and you have not started, and I would rather you did it badly in a hurry than well in a year, and I am not going to be standing in this shop in a year saying that you were careful.* And the last line of the volume: a man who cannot be released by any person has put the only two dates in this account where nobody is required to read them, and neither of them is his, and one of them is a Wednesday tonight and the other one is a Saturday night, and the bench will keep them for as long as the wood lasts, and the wood is very good.

---

## Volume 03, Batch 0001 — Chapters 101–110

**101 — What Came Back From Kestrel Hill.** Monday 20 October, the vestibule, Old Ward, eleven feet by nine. **The register of narrowings has SEVENTEEN entries and it had sixteen for the whole of August and September.** Entry 17, dated 2 October: Meter Room 3, Salter's Yard, a building of thirty-one rented rooms with no civic address, re-asserted at Tier 3 on the notice of 1 October, consent of no person required, holder field none — **and a last line that is not on the form, *entered on a public form by a person*, written in a hand because the space was there, and nobody in this city has ever once refused a space that was there.** A records supervisor has said out loud three times that the absence of a name box is a decision and not an oversight, and she is proved right inside eleven seconds: **he read a line that names no person and it gave him a street he had walked to twice and not gone up.** He asks one question at a counter — *Who entered line seventeen* — four words, and the counter tells him anybody can, and then tells him the rest unasked: the form went on on Wednesday morning, nobody saw, somebody with a lanyard at eight o'clock is possible. Calder tells him she does not know and does not want to and *a design is not a promise*. Wednesday 22 October, three publicly visible things and no fourth: the register at seventeen, **a notice of re-assertion stamped RECEIVED 21 OCT 2014 that came back with no name on it anywhere and no field for one**, and the two entries for Meter Room 3 read with each other. At four minutes past five the telephone rings and it is **BEVERLEY ANNIS, 54, principal of a managing agent with four hundred and ten flats, giving her name for the first time in five months.** She put *the named steward of the building* in the holder field herself, with a torch, on 3 June, because the point is not the payer's, and nobody told her what the class meant, and the person in the holder field does not know she is in the claim again and is not going to be told. **She also tells him the four meters are not going into that cupboard, and that she rang a shop because she could not sleep and there was nobody else she was allowed to ring.** He does not write it down — the first thing he has chosen not to write since 3 April. And the bench: **1 OCT and 11 OCT are still in the beech, a centimetre and a half high, and both of those dates have now gone — three weeks and eleven days — and nobody who is allowed to know has said what either one was.**

**102 — A Person Who Is Not Counted.** **CYNTHIA AKINTOLA, 61**, and the batch's only close POV outside Jonas Mercer, who is not in it. Wednesday 29 October, 6:20 p.m., the furniture workshop under Wren Court: two heaters, one working, eleven people in the cold half with their coats on, and a biscuit tin with a dog on it and a card under a rubber band with **nine hundred and eighty-six** on it, which is a number of Wednesdays and not money, and which is about four hundred and thirty pounds out of agreement with the tin and always has been, and which nobody has ever asked her about. A man of thirty-one with a clipboard and forty-one identical forms in his van asks three things: the state, the number of dwellings, the name of the person responsible. **The state she gives him, in about nine words, and he does not ask her one follow-up question and she notices. The number she refuses in nine seconds without raising her voice once, and the room does not move, and he writes NO in the second box in capitals, and it is the only capital letter on that page. The name she refuses and gives the reason: the person who holds it has a shopfront and a lease and is the only one of them anybody can write to, and she is not going to be the reason somebody wrote her name on a form in a van.** He asks, before he writes anything, whether she wants to hear what he is writing, and she says she can, and **he dictates the fourth page's method onto his own pad in his own file with no box on it — one thing, one time, one place, what was asked and what was given and what was refused, and a line saying what it is not — and does not know that he has written it.** Nobody argues, the room carries on, **the job does not.** And the private arithmetic underneath: the ninth seat said on a stair that she will not be paid until she is sixty-one and that after that she will not be, and Cynthia Akintola is sixty-one in the March, and there is nobody she could say that to, and she is not going to put it in a letter to a council. Four words in a corridor: *It's weeks. It was always weeks.*

**103 — Six Water Points And No Seat.** Friday 24 October, a counter, one question in seven words, and then he stops asking and a clerk reads the date off the back of an envelope upside down. **£11,280 a year: six water points on the lowland at Tier 1, £1,880 each, and a chamber and a spur and an outfall, paragraph 6.1 of the minute of 19 May, recorded as received and not as evidence, and nobody has been asked for any part of it.** And the wall: the layer draws nine service districts, eight named, the ninth not drawn, **so the lowland is not in the eight, so there is no seat for it, and there is no instrument in this city by which there could be one, and the reason is not an oversight and may not be fixed in this batch.** Tuesday 28 October, a Methodist hall, about eleven people, and the number goes on a table and nothing can be done with it. The room works out its own rule inside a minute — a seat may name a thing it answers for — and names **six shapes and no names**, and the minute says *six shapes were identified in discussion* and names none of them. **The sixth is named by the ninth seat: a green pillar with a bucket on a chain and a forty-slot board, at the top of a three-hundred-metre street with a canal on one side and boat sheds on the other, whose trap is gone and whose bank is gone — and she does not say where it is, and neither is the minute going to say where it is, and she has never said where it is to anybody in this city including the man against that wall, and she is not going to start in a hall.** A man of fifty-six who is not a member then says, across the hall, that he wrote *non-civic plant*, and **Jonas Mercer says one word — Yes — before he has decided whether it is going to, and the minute of 28 October, item 2, fourth paragraph, records that a person present, not on the agenda, stated that the classification was written by him, and does not contain his name, because there is no box for one.** In the pad, on a wall on a hill: *I have made a man who has never been asked anything into a man somebody can now connect to a classification. That is the first thing I have done in seven months that has made anybody findable, and it was not me doing it, and it is still me.*

**104 — The Reading Rule.** Friday 31 October, a counter, and then a corridor, and then a walk of forty-one minutes each way in the wind. **The reading rule of Thursday 21 August is seventy-one days old: where a party relies on either of the two entries for Meter Room 3, the other is to be read with it. It is not an amendment, it cures nothing and it changes no word, and it is the only procedural act available to that Committee and it is made under a general power about the manner in which returns are read.** Read together, the two entries say that a party relying on the named steward is also relying on a trust that was served with a ninety-day notice on 3 July and has answered a machine once and a person once, **and nobody has been required to tell it anything, and there is no instrument in this city that can require anybody to be aware of having been served.** The Committee can use the rule and it is the only body that can; a registrar can use it in a determination and nobody will let him; **a person at a meter cupboard, a managing agent and a woman in an apron cannot use it at all, and the room it works in is eleven feet by nine and all nine people in this borough who know the rule exists work in the building it was made in.** FRANCES TOLLEY, clerk of twenty-two years, types it, and says what it would take, and it is not money: *it would take a person being allowed to do it, in work time, in a way that is not a favour and not a favour from anybody, because if the managing agent's firm pays for it, it is a service, and a service is a rota with one name on it.* She cannot put it in a report, a job description, a rota, an establishment or a form. **On Wednesday 5 November she gives him one sheet of A4 in biro, no crest, no form number, no box, and walks it herself, forty-one minutes each way, and makes him walk with her because she is sixty-one and it is windy.** At Salter's Yard, **BEVERLEY ANNIS reads it on a step in the wind and refuses a copy, and the reason is correct and is the best argument in the chapter: *a copy of that page goes to the Aldbrook Trust, and the moment a clerk of sixty-three with a lever-arch file has it, the trust has a defence.*** Not a good one. A defence. And on the hill on the way back she says the thing she has been carrying: **on a Tuesday in November, if a man from field services comes in front of her at a counter, she is going to read him those two entries, and not ask him anything, and he is not going to have been told he was in it, and she is not going to be told it worked, because there is nothing to work with. And nobody is to put it in a pad.**

**105 — What The Audit Found.** Friday 7 November, a room over a shop in Gantry Ward, and Jonas Mercer is two streets away at a counter and is not in it. **CivicCore has audited itself: the nodes it holds, which is the thing it is paid to hold, from 1 September to 7 November.** It is good, and it is not neutral, and it is better than anything the borough has. It finds real faults, and that is the part nobody expected. **One hundred and six hinges with no last verified essential use. Forty-one places where the answer to *who answers for this* is a company, thirty-eight where it is a person, nine where it is a description of a person — and the nine are given as node references and the descriptions are not reproduced, because reproducing them would create a record of a person.** Four nodes paid for by a body that no longer existed: it did not disconnect them, it paid them itself, and Rook said that was the most expensive line item in the document and that nobody in that borough had ever written down the sentence *nobody is going to pay for it* before him. And a mortuary gate, node 6-44, the longest finding in the document at nine pages, maintained since about 2005 by a man who is not employed, is not a party, and whose name is in no field of no document: **this audit finds no fault at this node, and records that it is the most reliable arrangement it has encountered in four hundred and ten flats' worth of premises and that it is unlawful, and that both of those are correct.** The cover note, page one, that nobody wrote on purpose: *Every finding in this report is a finding about a node somebody is paying for. That is not a limitation of the audit. That is the audit.* Monday 10 November, he reads it standing up at a counter and the woman brings him a chair at half past two without being asked. **And at five past four the borough's housing office's letter to CivicCore goes past him on the internal post, and the counter tells him, having been asked nothing, that it is asking whether the hundred and twenty-eight rooms of the Carrow Lane association are in scope, that the answer is no, that it will be read out on the Thursday at half past two in the stock review with the chief officer of that association sitting nine feet away — and he stands on the pavement for forty seconds and does not ring her, because the only thing she can do about a true sentence is produce a list, and she has been refusing to produce a list since March and won in August.** He writes it down on the back of the fourth envelope in a hand that is not his best, and it is the shortest thing in the pad all year: *I would like somebody to explain to me the difference, because I have been using it as if it were a principle since April and it is not a principle, it is a preference of mine, and I am the only person in this city it is available to.*

**106 — A Letter Came Back.** Wednesday 12 November, ninety feet across a service alley, ninety seconds, four things in order. **The four meters are not going into the cupboard, the standing instruction of 14 April has been withdrawn, the meters have gone to a different block — a block of 1974 where everybody was at home in August — and there is a notice on the door under a sheet of A4 in a plastic wallet saying the freeholder intends to carry out a condition survey and report on the feasibility of restored supply.** Past tense, past tense, past tense, and then one future tense with a word in it that has never once in this city produced a restored anything. **IVY MARSDEN, 72, Block C room 14, four flights and no lift, has carried water for thirty rooms since 1998 and has been asked for a letter twice and refused twice in sixteen years, and does not know that a letter exists.** He goes up on Thursday the thirteenth, in daylight, at about half past three, with about nine minutes' notice, and does not telephone first, because a telephone call would make it an appointment and an appointment is a thing a person can decline, and he has come to give a person the right to decline a thing. **She would not have a copy, and the reason is not him.** She works out what the letter was for in about four minutes — he took from a hall in May to a bench in September — and then gives him the half he had not got: **the day a piece of paper says the buckets are the block's, the block is a thing that can be asked, and a thing that can be asked is a thing that has a landlord, and a thing that has a landlord is a thing that has a list.** He has made a Tuesday. And then, with her back to him at the corner container: **you have given me your name, that is the second thing that has happened to me in sixteen years and both of them were done by a man who thought he was being careful, and when I die somebody is going to ring a council and there is not going to be one line anywhere in that piece of paper that says I asked for it.** She lets him write that down. It is the first entry in the pad since 22 October longer than a sentence. **Nobody was saved.**

**107 — The Second Time Somebody Is Asked.** Monday 17 November, a counter, and a records supervisor who has refused a design four times this year. **She builds a page that is a drawing and not a photograph, with a number in it, because the first page could not be wrong and therefore could not be found wrong — a record of shapes with no name box on it is not a photograph and is not a service and cannot be argued with, and the only way to make it checkable is to make it specific, and the only way to make it specific is to name things, and naming things is what makes it a list.** FRANCES TOLLEY supplies the arithmetic in about four minutes, and it is the only arithmetic in this city anybody can check: **fifty-one nodes on the continuity schedule as at six o'clock on Monday the sixth of October 2014, thirty-eight parties answering them, thirteen shapes — and anybody in this borough may go to that office, ask for that extract on that date, and perform the subtraction, and if it is wrong they have a piece of paper with a date and a reference and a clerk's signature on it.** Wednesday 19 November, a records office, three people and a shut door: the page is written, **it goes out UNSIGNED, on the city's own paper, with no crest, no form number and no box for the person who put it together, and its last line says that absence out loud and says it was a decision — because the day she initials a sentence about a document it becomes a rule about documents, and in about four years there will be a form with her initials in the corner of it.** Nobody is required to be told they are in it, and that is the design. The number in November is bigger than the number in September and **the difference is four, and Calder can say what three of them are and will not say the fourth, and Jonas Mercer will not say it either, in a room with three people in it, and the reason is that a sentence with a lot of people in it about a thing that has no address is a direction.** The chair receives it, declines to adopt it, and asks for the declining to be minuted. It is published in the third week of November and about four hundred people stand in front of it on the first morning and work out where they are. **And the fourth of the four is a meter room a person could walk to, on a public document, naming nobody — and it can be walked to, and that is what the woman who wrote the page thought about in a corridor on 19 November, standing up, with her coat on, before she had said goodnight to anybody.**

**108 — The Man Who Classifies Streets As Plant.** Friday 21 November, the Methodist hall, and the volume outline's unpaid Section E line is paid, against him. **The seventh seat has been empty for forty-four days, since Wednesday 8 October, when Kathleen Ferris, 33, night bus controller, wrote four lines on the back of a bus timetable in about eleven minutes and gave them to the woman who keeps the minutes, and nobody has read them out.** Ibrahim Sesay, 51, caretaker, Tarn Street school, thirty-one years, second seat, stands up to ask one question, which nobody asked him to do: would you tell this room whether you wrote them. He says no, and then yes, and then **says the whole of it in nineteen seconds, to the floor and not to anybody: 6-L/14, non-civic plant, written on 14 March at 12:41 in a records room on the second floor and initialled, and lodged into the series six file on 22 March under a reporting officer number that is not his, by Royden Achebe, forty-nine, field services, who did not read it before he lodged it and has never said so.** The room does three correct things with it in forty seconds — the ninth seat stops a man treating it as a confession, one man refuses to put eight months of being angry at a form into the same drawer as his four lines, and the eighth seat calls it a liability and is right: *if he can call a street plant then he can call a plant room plant, and if somebody in this room had put him in that chair in September then I would not be on this board, and neither would Dermot, and neither would Winifred, and it would not be a board of nine people who are each answerable for a thing.* And a fourth thing, about somebody who is not in the room: **you have put his name to a sentence about his own conduct and nobody in this room can tell him, and the difference from August is that a man of forty-four cannot be asked one question about any of it by anybody, ever, and a man of forty-nine can be asked one tomorrow morning, and one of them cannot be.** Then the ninth seat asks him for the seventh seat, as a person and not as a body, and he refuses in eleven words: **A man who calls a street plant must not be findable.** Monday 24 November, four pages at a counter, and a minute that does not contain his name. **Nine requests in two days, eight of them journalists, and the counter woman will not be the reason a man who is not on the agenda finds out he is on the front of a paper.** In the shop, Bea Osunde tells him he has started, that it took him eight months, that the first name he said out loud was his own, and then names three people who need the harder half: *being findable is not the same as being useful, and you have done the easy one and it took you eight months and I have watched all three of them get older this year while you were getting round to yourself.*

**109 — Six Vacancies.** Wednesday 26 November, a corridor, then a Methodist hall, then a counter on Friday the twenty-eighth. **The person who empties a seat in this chapter is not in this chapter**, and he has not been asked and came at twenty-five past five and stood against the left-hand wall, and the minute records that a person was present at the invitation of nobody. Frances Tolley says no before he has said what the question is, and gives the reason: *you have asked me nine questions since March and every single one of them has been about a document. It is a pattern, and in about four years it is going to be the only thing anybody in this borough knows about you.* In the hall: nine people, nine chairs, one chair empty for forty-nine days, and **four of the nine have said they will stop if it turns into a job.** Stanley Umeh, 58, market porter and keeper of a lock-up at Bellow Market for thirty years, says it at nine minutes past six — *Right. I'm going* — and says the whole of it: he has had a shutter and a padlock in this borough since about 1984 and nobody has ever asked him what the padlock is for, and he is not stopping because of anybody, and he is the fourth of them who has said it and he will not be in a room on a Friday evening with three-quarters of himself. **Nobody tries to keep him. Nobody argues, nobody asks a second question, nobody says *are you sure*, and the minute records all of it.** Ines Tarrant, 44, car park supervisor, eleven years, says she meant it too and has not stopped, and asks to be asked why, and nobody is able to ask her. Winifred Crabbe, 62, linen supervisor, nineteen years, says the thing nobody was willing to say: a body which cannot be required to do anything cannot be told to stop, and Stanley has just gone, **and that is not a contradiction, and she wants it in the minute that it is not a contradiction, because in about four years somebody is going to write one of those two things at the top and the other at the bottom and it will read as a hypocrisy, and it is not a hypocrisy, it is the design.** The ninth seat then says out loud, as an observation and not a motion, what Calder wrote on 2 June, and adds: **two chairs are empty now, and that is not a step towards anything, it is a Tuesday.** Friday at the counter, two lines and the arithmetic: **seven of nine**, and it stood at nine of nine from 4 September to 8 October and at eight of nine for forty-nine days, and it has no power to fill either vacancy and will not seek one. And then the counter woman, unasked, having noticed: the ninth seat read the minute, was in the room when it was read out, took a copy, **and has not come to the counter.** He stood outside it for about fifty minutes on the Friday morning and then gave up. **He has not rung her and he is not going to ring her, and that is not a rule he has made for her sake. In about four years there will be a piece of paper that says a man telephoned a woman to ask her whether she had read a minute.**

**110 — The Floor, Again.** Monday 1 December, the vestibule, eleven feet by nine, and **the register has NINETEEN entries**: entry 18, a riser space at the rear block, Carrow Lane, placed for a condition survey on 29 October that lapsed on 12 November, no answer to a return, narrowed on 14 November; entry 19, a yard light, no party, no last verified essential use, **maintenance is a bulb, and the bulb is out.** Three times in nine weeks, every one of the three correct, and a records supervisor says the true thing in a corridor: **a register that grows when nobody is hurt is a register with a motive in it and a register somebody can hold over you, and if anybody in this borough ever uses that board against anybody they will not use the meter room, they will use the bulb.** He was in the room for one of the three. He and Calder do the inventory, and he says out loud the one he did himself: on 21 November he said the six words were his in front of ten people, and one of the things he said with them was the name of a man who was not in the room, and that man cannot answer it and no instrument in this city will make anybody tell him. **"It is not the same and it is not smaller," she says, "and that is what I want said in a corridor and not in a minute."** Monday afternoon, a Methodist hall, and a sheet of A4 face up on the trestle table that came **through the door of a Methodist hall on Friday 28 November**, because a published minute says where a body of nine sits, and a woman with a key found it on the Saturday and carried it in her bag for three days and told nobody. It is signed, and it asks whether the stand at the bottom of Quarry Street is one of the six, and it says *I am not asking you for anything, there is no form on this and I know there is no form on this*, and it is signed at the bottom in a hand that is not a clerk's. The woman who keeps the minutes says what a letter is, in the right order: **it is not a document, so it cannot be filed; not a form, so it cannot be refused; not a minute, so it cannot be minuted; and it is addressed to a room, so it cannot be delivered.** He asks his one question and it is five words and it is her name, and nobody has ever asked her that, and her name is in a box on a sheet and there is no other box in this borough that says who wrote a thing down. Wednesday 3 December, twenty to seven in the morning, the bottom of Quarry Street: **she stops with the trolley, and says there is a date on that stand and no person on it and that is the only deal there is, and that on Friday she put her name on a piece of paper on purpose because a room is not a record, and she had worked that out before she wrote a word of it. And he tells her what he is going to do, and the day and the hour, because a person who keeps a date is entitled to know when it stops being one, and he does not ask her for anything.** That evening, about eleven people, half past five: the letter is read out and is not minuted and that is said in the room and minuted. He is asked to chair it and refuses. **And at 18:41 he says `Bernice Ofori` out loud, four syllables, nine seconds, once, to the floor.** Nobody asked him a second question and nobody asked whether it was correct. The ninth seat says three things: we did not go and get her and we could not have; nobody in that room asked her for anything and nobody is going to; and nobody is to go down there on Monday. **The minute says a name was stated, once, at 18:41, and does not contain it, and records that there is no box for one and that this is a decision and not an oversight. It went out on Friday 5 December and it is a public document and the name is not in it, and the name is in about eleven people, and there is no instrument in this borough that can un-say a thing in a room and there is one that can un-write it.** Then, because nobody asked him anything and he was not on the agenda, he does the thing he has never done in a room and counts what was left standing, out loud, in the order he does it: **seven of nine, two chairs empty, four of the nine who will stop if it turns into a job, a lowland that is not on the layer and cannot be seated, six shapes named in no document, four tonnes of stone and about £1,180 owed to nobody, a second tray on a shelf with the same answer since 29 May, a man of forty-four who signed a line in ninety seconds and cannot be asked, and a Sunday coming — the Saint Orra west term expiring at six o'clock in the morning on Sunday the fourth of January, terms six days, the rota not rebuilt, the gate on its local term, the handover on the Saturday night before, and no date written down anywhere for that Saturday night.** It is eleven feet by nine and it is the same eleven feet by nine it was in July and **none of it has added a single hinge to a board in Gantry Ward**, and he asks for that minuted as an observation and not as an achievement.

---

## Volume 03, Batch 0002 — Chapters 111–120

**111 — The Second Time Somebody Is Asked, Again.** Thursday 11 December 2014, a kitchen off a landing, Wren Court, Salter's Yard, Tideglass — **the second close POV in Volume 03, on an unpaid person who is on no board — the third of the volume so far, with Chapter 0102 and Chapter 0114's Nina Calder, and the rule is not one per volume and not one per batch either but rather that each one has to be spent on a person who cannot be got into a room.** Eight feet by six, two gas rings and one does not light, the cloth off because the cloth goes on a Sunday. **A ROOM HAS A MINUTE. A KITCHEN DOES NOT, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE OF THE CHAPTER.** She is YOLANDA FOFANA, 54, named steward of Wren Court since about 2001, unpaid since 4 July because thirty-one rooms stopped paying a participation fee in the same week in June, and the only one of the nine anybody can write to. He announces that he is going to use one question and that a kitchen is not a public room, because if he does not tell her she cannot decide what to do about it. **She does the thing she has done four times in fourteen years and does it before he has finished: she does not refuse him. She took about four seconds and said: *you have asked me that in a shop in September and in a records office and in a hall on a Friday with nine chairs in it and two of them empty, and this is the fourth time, and the answer is the same four times and nobody has ever asked me whether I would rather you did not ask.*** And: *nobody in this borough is permitted to argue with me about a thing I have said no to, and that is not the same as nobody arguing with me because I would win.* **The refusal of the batch, in a kitchen, with no minute and no room: A PERSON WHO IS FINDABLE IS A PERSON WHO CAN BE ASKED. AND A PERSON WHO CAN BE ASKED HAS TO HAVE AN ANSWER READY.** Fourteen years of practice: the answer is always no, always about nine seconds, always about a thing and not about her, always the same no, and nobody can ever tell whether she has thought about it. **AND SHE HAS NOT GOT ONE READY FOR THIS, and she is not going to get one ready in a room, and it is the same refusal as the twenty-fourth of September and the reason is the same reason and it stands.** Tomas Renn's question is NOT answered and is NOT manufactured. She refuses to discuss the minute of 3 December, because once two people have discussed a minute it is a thing that happened and then somebody can ask her what she thinks of it. He puts the pad face down on her table, which is the only thing about him she has never mentioned. The letter from 28 November is in her bag in a handbag and it stays there for five and a half weeks. **Nobody in that building of thirty-one rented rooms may be told that she is a door, and she is not the only door in that building, and she is the only person in it anybody can write to, and she does not know the difference and has said twice that she will not be told.** Neither of them thanked the other and there was no word between them at the end of it.

**112 — Two Lines On A Thursday.** Thursday 18 December 2014, half past two, the stock review, a room with a book in it, about nineteen people. **Every written question in that borough goes in that book and every answer gets read, and the reader is a person, and the person is DOREEN WHITLOCK, about sixty, eleven years, and the book has a column for her.** He asks his one question in the corridor at about ten minutes past two — *Who reads the answers out* — and none in the room, and he has not warned her, and the not-warning was decided on a pavement in November and is the one he wrote down as going to cost the most. Item seven. Reference HC/CR/1147, received 27 November. **THE ANSWER IS TWO LINES AND IT IS TRUE:** *the association is not a party to CivicCore's service agreements and its premises are not within the scope of the audit.* Nine people in the room understand the whole of it and about ten do not, and the ten who do not are the ones who will remember it in four years. **And Marguerite Oyelaran says no to the chair, without standing up and without waiting to be asked, and asks for the answer to be minuted in the borough record AS A THING THAT HAS BEEN SAID IN A ROOM, so that the word *scope* will stand in two files in two buildings, and says the reason out loud, and the reason is the sentence of the batch: *because a thing that is wrong in one place and right in another is a thing that gets corrected in four years by whoever is left.*** The chair has no form of words for it and she asks for that to be recorded too. **It was the right thing and it is the worst thing that could have happened to her, and both of those are true at once: she now has a date against her name and the word *disputes* in a borough record, and a list is the only instrument this borough has ever offered her, and she has made herself the person most likely to be asked for one.** Four minutes later a **one hundred and six** comes out of the book sideways out of a June return, and a member of the room knows the August figure is **ninety-two**, and the difference is **fourteen**, said out loud by a man who is not a bad man and is a man who reads. She does not explain it and cannot be made to. **The difference is a floor she has moved in four months, not fourteen dwellings; there is now a hundred and six in a housing file and a hundred and six in a borough record, and a number of fourteen in a housing file reads as fourteen households and is not fourteen anything, and there is no form anywhere on which anybody could write that.** The register has not moved since 26 November and it is the third week of December. Monday 22 December: a sheet of A4 in an envelope arrives on a records counter from somebody who is not a records officer and goes back in the same envelope with nothing written on it, and the request that asks for a column does not arrive until the Friday morning post of 2 January, from a man of sixty-one who has asked at that counter once before. Monday 22 December: a sheet of A4 in an envelope arrives on a records counter, and it is the first request anybody has made of that board in a year that asks for a box.

**113 — The Man Who Can Be Asked.** Monday 22 December into Wednesday 24 December. **A counter, one question in eleven words — *what would it take to reach Royden Achebe without a record* — and the answer is nothing, and the answer is exact:** a post, a telephone on a switchboard with a queue and a person and a list, a team, a building, a reception with a book, a reporting officer number that is Jonas Mercer's and is on a piece of paper a man of forty-four signed in ninety seconds, and a duty described in two files that do not know each other exists. **EVERY ROUTE TO HIM IS A DOCUMENT AND A PERSON WHO WILL REMEMBER IT.** A man of forty-four is a wall and a man of forty-nine is a door, and *a door is not a favour, a door is a thing you walk people through, and in about four years somebody is going to walk somebody through him with a piece of paper, and it will be you that they say did it.* He does not go on the Monday afternoon and does not go on the Tuesday. **He does not go into the building.** He stands on the first-floor landing outside a fire door for about ten minutes with nothing in his hands and twice somebody comes out of the door and neither of them looks at him and he does not look at either of them. He counts the four routes and does not use one, and then he writes down a fifth thing that is not a route at all. **Wednesday the twenty-fourth, the shop, and the hardest refusal in the batch, and the reason it is the hardest is that nobody in the shop was arguing with him and nobody was asking him for anything:** an apology from the man who named you in a room you were not in has to be said in a room, and there is no room in this borough in which it can be said that does not also become a record, and the only kind of room left is somebody's kitchen, and he has not got one and is not entitled to one, and turning up at a man's door is what he did to a woman of seventy-two in November and it cost her the last thing she had left. **He would rather be the man who did it in a room than the man who did it in a file. In a room it happened to him and he could answer back and it would be over. In a file it is a handle.** She tells him to say the last part to himself four times a day for a week. He writes **the reason and not the apology** on Monday 29 December on the bench, and she tells him that is what a pad is for: it is his, there is no box on it anywhere, and that is also the point. **Three men, none told anything, none resolved, none a rescue:** the man of forty-four who cannot be asked, ever; the man of forty-nine who can be asked and is not; and a man of forty-eight who did a weekly verification for about eleven weeks in the autumn with no name against it, stopped in October, said in a corridor in August that a job in November has a department in it, **and has not been asked about it since and nobody has rung him, and the autumn compliance report is out with a paragraph number on it in front of four hundred and twenty women.** He did not use the keypad. He asked no question. **There are no Mondays left in the term: it expires at six o'clock on Sunday 4 January, in eleven days.**

**114 — A Register With A Motive In It.** Friday 2 January into Sunday 4 January 2015, and **the batch's second close POV, on the only person in the city who designed the board, a records supervisor who is paid and is on no board either**. **NINA CALDER, about fifty, nineteen years in that office and designing lists since 1999 — two different numbers and she has never confused them in public — and on 2 January somebody puts a sheet of A4 on her counter in an envelope and asks her for a column, and a column is a box that has learned to write.** The board has NINETEEN entries, sixteen since September and still since 4 August, and it has not moved since 26 November, which is thirty-seven days and the longest since August, and she has been right for five weeks and the bulb is still a bulb. The letter is addressed to her by name, which is not a thing anybody in this borough does, and the reference in the corner is wrong. **From M. FURNEAUX, 61, resident, the rear block, Carrow Lane, and it is the politest and the most sensible letter anybody has put in front of her in a year and the most dangerous document anybody in this borough has handed to that counter since March, because he is NOT ASKING FOR A NAME. He is asking for a REASON: one line, free text, against each of the nineteen, not a drop-down, not a category, in the hand of the person who decided it, dated, because in about four years he would like to be able to explain that board to somebody who is frightened of it and cannot, because nobody has ever written down why.** Jonas Mercer comes in at half past nine and asks his one question in eleven words — *would a reason column have to be filled in by hand* — and the answer is yes, and that it means there are only about four people in this borough who could fill in that column for nineteen entries and none of them can be made to. **A REASON COLUMN IS A SENTENCE COLUMN AND A SENTENCE COLUMN IS A NAME BOX WITH THE SERIAL NUMBERS LEFT OFF. THE SERIAL NUMBERS STAY BECAUSE THE SERIAL NUMBERS ARE A MACHINE. THE SENTENCES ARE ABOUT US.** She refuses, at a counter, in a public room, in the right order, in about eleven seconds, in four numbered parts, **and it is the seventh, and it stands, and nobody may come back to it**: the name box stays away by decision; a sentence is a thing a person is found in; the only instrument this borough offers is a list and she has watched that go wrong twice this year; and **a form is the last thing a person writes and the first thing an institution keeps.** Eleven seconds and not one, because one word is a door closing and eleven words is a door with a reason in it. **And she tells him, on the way out, that nobody has written any of it down, and neither has he, and there are two of them in this borough who could put it in a file before lunch and neither is going to, and that is the whole of what this borough has instead of a rule.** The envelope goes back on Monday 5 January with nothing in it and a stamp of the same denomination. Her arithmetic on the Friday night: nineteen sentences in four hands in a room eleven feet by nine over four years, by people who will have forgotten what they were thinking about. She writes the six refusals out once and crosses the list and writes under it: *seven. Stop counting.* Saturday: nothing happened, and that is the first time in five weeks. **Sunday the fourth at ten past two in the morning, the vestibule, and Jonas Mercer stands in front of the board for about eleven minutes and does not touch it, and the blank line is blank, and the two conditions are still sixteen inches apart and unreconciled, and the term has three hours and fifty minutes left in it, and the handover was on the night before at ten o'clock and there is no date written down anywhere for that Saturday night.**

**115 — The Smallest Possible Room, Before Six O'Clock.** Saturday 3 January at four minutes to ten at night, in the rain at the bottom of a service road where there is no pavement, and then Sunday 4 January from about half past four in the morning. **He is four hundred yards away and he is not going to come, and the reason is not a rule:** if he is in that corridor on the last night before a term expires then two tired people can say *he was there* in about four years, and that is a rota with one name on it, and the last time anybody in this city learned that it cost four tonnes of stone and about eleven hundred pounds. **He does not telephone to ask whether it went, because ringing is the same as going.** Fifty minutes on foot in the rain, about two degrees. **The smallest possible room in this city is not eleven feet by nine. It is a waiting area off a corridor on level two at Saint Orra west with four chairs in it and a machine for hot water that has not worked since about August, and it has no door, and it has been the room where this happens since the first week of April.** The term in force has an hour and thirty-five minutes left; it was read at ten o'clock on Saturday 27 December and began at seven on Sunday the twenty-eighth and ran six days. **Between six o'clock and seven o'clock there is no term at all. That is not a fault anybody has been told about. It is arithmetic. Once a week, every Sunday morning, for an hour, there is a door that exists because a person decided to be there, and in about four years somebody is going to write a schedule for that hour and the schedule will be good.** He stands eleven feet back in the corridor, out of the light, for two hours and five minutes, and **asks no question, because there was nobody there to ask it of.** **EZRA MBEKI and TREVOR NUNN, 58, a night porter, nineteen years, who is not on a roster because the roster does not exist.** On the wall: the rota that has not been rebuilt since 2 April, the 11:00–13:30 row empty in the same typeface as every other row; `B. OSEI. NO.` / *no reason I can give* / *(no reason given)*, the second line in somebody else's hand, and he has asked twice that it not be looked at; and Priya Ranganathan's card in a plastic sleeve because she put it there. **Somebody puts a clipboard on the fourth chair — eleven rows, the first four written on at some point in October by a man who then stopped — and he does not touch it, and the reason he gives is that a log with one name in it is a rota with one name in it, and the day he signs it the man who stopped in October is a man who let somebody down.** At six o'clock the term expires and nothing happens. At a quarter to seven a woman comes up the ramp and the gate goes, because somebody is standing at it. **A MAN WHO CANNOT BE REQUIRED TO DO ANYTHING IS THE ONLY PERSON IN THAT BUILDING WHO CAN BE THERE WITHOUT BEING A SERVICE. That is worth nothing and it is the only thing that works. It is not the first time in this account that a thing has been got right by nobody writing it down.** The forty minutes of 15 April is time until the family knew and is not the time the door took, and no other figure of minutes is going to appear in this account, ever. **The next expiry is 06:00 on Sunday 11 January and the handover is on the Saturday night before and there is no date written down anywhere for that Saturday night either.**

**116 — Six Of Them.** Tuesday 13 January into Thursday 15 January 2015, a Methodist hall, in daylight for the first time. **Winifred Crabbe asks for the price in the voice of a woman who has read a laundry list aloud for nineteen years: six water points on the lowland at Tier 1, £1,880 each, £11,280 a year, paragraph 6.1 of the minute of 19 May, recorded as received and not as evidence, and nobody has been asked for any part of it.** **Gloria Nye does the conversion out loud: that is one boiler in a small flat, the cheapest anybody quoted her in two years was £1,790 and she would not recommend it, and the reason the six of them cannot be bought at all is not that the people who need them are poor — the reason is that we are not on the map.** And Dermot Fahey and Oren Pell: **a district that cannot pay has a seat and a person in it and has not thereby lost its vote; the reason there is no seat for the lowland is that a node with no party is not a party and a place is not a person, and there is no instrument in this city by which a ninth district could be constituted in a Methodist hall by anybody in a bad mood — and the registrar says that reason is correct, has been correct since the first of March, and is not going to be fixed.** Jonas Mercer asks no question in the room at all. **And a man of fifty-eight comes in at ten past three and asks to be counted: ABU BAKAYOKO, twenty-six years at a stand at the top of the market, five in the morning, a bucket, two urns and no drain, a man paid on Fridays to empty them twice a day since about 1989 whose name he does not know and has never asked.** He does not want a seat, money, or even a number. **He wants a line in a minute saying that a thing exists at a place where a thing is needed, because in about four years somebody is going to come with a piece of paper about that market and there is going to be nothing on the table that says anything was there.** He gives the state of five of the six in about two minutes without stopping and it is the best testimony that hall has had in four months: a standpipe that has not run since Friday 28 November and a bowser at four pounds a bucket, a trough frozen since 22 December, a stand with a bucket on a chain and a board of forty slots that works because a man puts money in the slot and washes the bucket, a stand on a school wall that runs on a timer for forty minutes at a quarter to eight, and a tap by the yard that has been dry since the summer. **AND HE STOPS, AND HE REFUSES THE SIXTH OUT LOUD BEFORE ANYBODY ASKS HIM, and asks for the refusal to be written down, and the reason is the volume's method in one sentence: there is somebody on that street who has told the county that nothing is there and she is right to and he is not going to be the person who takes it off her.** Nobody asked him. Then a man near the back asks Jonas Mercer whether this is a seventh shape, and **he says the only thing he says all afternoon, and it is not a question, and it is eleven words: A SHAPE HAS TO HAVE A PLACE YOU CAN POINT AT. THAT HAS A PERSON IN IT. NO SEVENTH SHAPE IS MADE, AND THE SEVENTH IS NOT RESOLVED.** The body works through it and finds there is no party to serve, no seat to refer it to, no money, and no power to require any person to do anything. **There is no chair of that body and there never was.** Ibrahim Sesay and Oren Pell both say *nothing*, and the ninth seat says: *we write down that we did nothing, in the correct words, and we do not apologise for it.* **The minute went out on Thursday 15 January and it is public and may be had at a counter for nothing, and it has no number in it and no name in it, and it records that this body has done nothing, in the correct words, and that the doing of nothing is not a defect in the minute.** One Lattice notice in the batch, on the wall above that counter: **CONTINUITY SCHEDULE — TIER 1. SIX NODES. £1,880 PER NODE PER YEAR. £11,280 PER YEAR. NO PARTY ANSWERING. TERM TO 06:00, SUNDAY 7 JUNE 2015. THIS NOTICE MAY NOT BE MATCHED AGAINST ANY LIST OF ADDRESSES.** And the hook: the minute goes out with no number in it and no name in it, and there is a woman of about thirty-four in this borough who has been counted in a room and has not been written down, and the two of those are different problems.

**117 — Nobody Is Going To Go Down There On Monday.** Monday 19 January into Wednesday 21 January. **A name said out loud in a room cannot be un-said, produced, checked, argued with, minuted, published, attached to a claim, or found by anybody who is not one of the eleven people who were in that room. There is no ninth item and there is not going to be one.** In about four years somebody is going to come with a piece of paper about that stand and there is not going to be one thing anybody in this borough can put beside it, **and whether that is the best outcome available or the worst one is not going to be said this year.** Nobody has been asked and nobody is going to be asked and nobody has gone down there. And then the post comes to the Methodist hall on the Monday, **because the hall is the only address there is:** a printed slip on borough stock with a crest, a reference, an issue date and a form number, addressed to *the person who submitted correspondence to the Cooperative Steward Board*, care of the hall, because the return address on a letter that went through a door is the door. It says correspondence is not admitted to a sitting unless the writer is present, and that if you are not present your correspondence will be treated as withdrawn, and it carries **three boxes — *I will attend / I withdraw it / I have not been asked to do either* — and a line for a name and a line for a date, and the third of those is the only correct answer on the sheet and there is nowhere on the sheet to put it.** Nobody in that hall sent it; the body cannot require a person to attend a sitting it is not required to hold. The woman who keeps the minutes puts it in a tray under the trestle table, writes a second unsigned handwritten note beside it, leaves the two of them on the table at about half past three on the Wednesday, shuts the hall and goes out, and tells nobody. **At about twenty past four on Wednesday 21 January, in daylight, in a hall with the heating off and nobody in it, a woman of thirty-four comes in through the door she put a letter through on 28 November.** Nobody went and got her. Nobody telephoned her. There is no number in this borough for anybody to telephone. Jonas Mercer is four hundred yards away at a counter and does not know. She reads both pieces of paper standing up, puts no mark in any of the three boxes, and **DOES NOT WRITE ON THE PRINTED ONE — a sheet with a crest and a form number and a reference is a document and her name is not going at the top of a document because nobody asked her for it.** She writes on the second one instead, which is unsigned, has no heading, no box and no line for anything and cannot be used to show anybody anything at all: her name and the street and the date, in her own hand, in biro, in a line across the top like somebody signing a form — the first time in eleven years those four things have been on one piece of paper together, and she did it on purpose and in daylight — and then nine seconds that cost her nothing: I HAVE NOT WITHDRAWN ANYTHING. I ASKED A QUESTION. THERE IS NO SITTING TO ATTEND AND NOBODY HAS ASKED ME TO ATTEND ONE.** She leaves both pieces on the table, takes nothing with her — her own November letter was still in a handbag in a kitchen four hundred yards away — and goes out at about ten to five, on a bus she had planned herself. The minute-taker comes back at eleven minutes past five, reads the top of it twice, puts the printed slip back in the tray where a printed thing goes, and **puts that whole sheet — four lines of the minute-taker's biro with her own four above them — in a plastic wallet with a rubber band in the drawer of the trestle table, and shuts the drawer, and there is no key, and there is no list of what is in any drawer in that building.** He is told on Friday 23 January in a shop, in about nine seconds, by the ninth seat, **and he asks her nothing about any of it and spends his one question of that morning in the shop about two minutes later, once, and she tells him the letter is still in her bag, that for seven weeks it was the only piece of paper in this borough with that name on it, and that she is not going to be told where the other one is and neither is he.** The one question of the chapter is asked in the shop: *whether a person may hold a document about herself in this borough.* And the answer is not an answer: **a person may put whatever she likes in her own bag; that has never needed an instrument and it is not going to get one.** **There are now two pieces of paper in this city with her name on them and neither of them is a document, neither has a crest, a form number, a reference or a box, neither can be used for anything, and NEITHER SAYS ONE WORD ABOUT A STAND AT THE BOTTOM OF A STREET, which is why in about four years a person with a folder will be able to put nothing on the table at all. A document is a lever; she has put her own name on two pieces of paper that are not documents; she is thirty-four; there was nobody in that hall; she was given nothing and she asked for nothing; her name is not on the printed slip and may not be put on it.**

**118 — The Price Of The Rule.** Thursday 22 January into Friday 23 January. A corridor, second floor, at about four in the afternoon, and the counter at about eleven that morning is where he found out. **She says no before he has said what the question is, and it is the fifth time, and it is that one of them is the whole of what is left of him as a person that she can still work with, and it is about four inches.** Three about a document and one in a corridor in November before he had said what it was, and this is the fifth, and she corrects his pad: a reason is a thing you give once, and the second time it is a policy and she is not a policy. He asks whether the sheet is still in her pocket. **She says no, and then tells him the truth inside the same nine seconds, and says both of those are allowed and he is not going to enjoy the second one.** And then she gives the price out loud, to the one person in the city who cannot use it, and it is the first time anybody has: **there is no way at all to say that she has not done it on a given day, and she would not want them to be able to, and on any given Tuesday nobody in the world can tell whether a reading rule was read in a corridor in Kestrel Hill or in Tideglass, including her. Forty-one minutes each way, on her own time, in the wind, at sixty-one, since 5 November, and nobody knows and nobody is to know.** She can put it in a rota, a form, an establishment, a job description, an edition, a standing order or a handbook, and every one of those is the obvious solution and every one of them makes it a service. **AND WHAT A CORRIDOR IS FOR, which is the answer to the question he has been asking her since June: a corridor is the only place in this borough that has no minute in it, no agenda, no box, no name, no gallery and no publication, and a rule is a door, and a door in a corridor is a door that can be opened by a person on her own time for a person who turns up, and there is no other room in this city where that sentence is true.** **THE READING RULE HAS BEEN USED, ONCE, on an undated day in January, in a corridor at that counter, at about eleven in the morning, in about eleven seconds, on a man of forty-four who was not told he was in it and who does not know what any of the words mean.** She is not going to say whether it worked and nobody is going to ask her. He is not going to be told. **Jonas Mercer finds out because the man says four words at the counter to somebody else on Thursday 22 January at about a quarter past eleven: `I was read to.`** The clerk tells him nothing and tells him that if he asks she will answer, because a counter is required to answer. **He did not ask. It is the fifth thing put in front of him about a person in eight months and he has stopped keeping a list, which is the only progress he has made.** In the pad on Friday: *I think what I have got is that a person was read two sentences and was able to say no and did not.* And: *I have written in this pad eleven times since March that I do not know, and eleven is a number of times and it is not a number of people.* On Friday afternoon he walks to the bottom of a service road where there is no pavement, **and does not go up the path**, because there is no reason she would recognise. **The sixth water point has a trap packed and a bank gone and cannot be cleared by anybody, ever; the three positions are on that kitchen wall; the fourth line has a ring of pencil round a blank space; Margo Doré is not angry and has not forgiven him and has not been asked anything since 1 June; and there are pumps for sale in four shops in this borough at about £240 and not one of them has ever been in a van on that street, and it is not a matter of money and it never was.**

**119 — A Service Floor.** Monday 26 January 2015, ten past nine, the vestibule, ground floor, the Old Ward. **The smallest room in this city in which anybody can be said to work, and it is the smallest rather than the largest because a person cannot stand in it comfortably with another person in it and has to turn sideways, and that has turned out in nine months to matter more than anything about the size.** Here is the whole of the floor: a counter, eleven feet, with a slot for post that has been **empty since the eighteenth of July** — not full, not emptied; a board at the end of it with **nineteen entries and a blank line**; two conditions sixteen inches apart above it; a clerk from the second floor who comes down at about ten and about three; **and about nine people a day, four on a wet one, between four and eleven since 4 September, counted on the back of a hand and not going in a book.** That is the only question he asks in that room, and a vestibule with a public corridor through it is a public room. **Oren Pell comes down at a quarter to ten and does not come in, because a registrar who comes into a room of eleven feet by nine turns it into a room he was in**, and gives the two sentences the volume runs on: *a thing that was going to happen anyway now has to be argued for in public, repeatedly, by people who are not paid to argue* — Rook's sentence about him, written in August, and he was right, and it made him expensive on purpose — and *a clause that cannot be challenged by a person cannot require them to*; and then the third: **a list of who needs it is a list of who exists, and in four years the list is in a drawer with a date on it, and the date is the day somebody asked, and the asking is the harm.** And: *you have spent eight months arguing with that sentence and you have not refuted it and you are not going to.* **And then a man with a bowser and a man on the bowser comes in off the street, and he does not want a list, he wants a state, and he has a Tuesday he cannot get back.** The clerk: *a counter is required to answer and a counter is not required to make one.* **AND IT TAKES ABOUT ELEVEN SECONDS. The two are running and two are dry and one is frozen, and the standpipe at the top of the market runs when somebody has paid the water, and the stand with a bucket on a chain and a board of forty slots works because a man puts money in the slot and washes the bucket, **and she has never seen that man's name on anything and he is not on anything**, and the one by the yard is dry and there is a person who is not a party and no answer has been received from anybody and that is in a file and the file is the whole of what the records office knows. She writes a date on a slip and turns it round and puts it on the counter. And the sixth is not in it, and he did not tell her and she did not ask him and there is no box on it for it.** Jonas Mercer understands in about four seconds what has happened and it is the whole of the answer to the question this volume has been asking since the first of October, and it is one sentence long and **it does not scale: a service floor is a counter, a person at it, a state asked for instead of a list, and a date.** No scheme, no form, no box, no reference. **It cannot be argued with, because there is nothing in it to argue with. That is the point, and it will be argued with: any person may come back to that counter on any morning and say the state of the market standpipe is not that, and be right, and be argued with, and get a second date.** **And the man does not get his Tuesday back, and the water still costs four pounds a bucket, and the man at the forty-slot board is still not findable from any record in this city, and a person who is not counted is not a person who is helped, and this chapter has not argued with that sentence and is not going to.**

**120 — What Is On The Floor.** Wednesday 28 January 2015, about eleven in the morning, and **the board has moved its meeting into the vestibule on purpose, in daylight, and there is no chair, because a body of nine seats has one power and a chair is not one of the three parts of it, and the ninth seat has been going through the order of things since July because she is the only one of the nine anybody can write to, and that is not a chair and nobody is to make it into one. Nine people in eleven feet by nine, standing sideways, with the woman who keeps the minutes at the end of the counter with her sheet because there is no table in the room.** He asks no question in that room and there is no question in it that was his. The room begins, as it does now, with the things that are true: the lowland cannot be seated and the reason is correct; a man of forty-four cannot be asked anything, ever; a woman of thirty-four is in eleven people and no document and nobody is to go down there; a woman of seventy-two has a sheet of A4 in a plastic wallet on her door and is a great deal more frightened and nobody has gone up those stairs since 14 November; a woman of sixty-one has a number of Wednesdays on a card under a rubber band in a biscuit tin and nobody is going to ask her about it; **and the ninth seat is the only person in a building of thirty-one rented rooms that anybody can write to, and nobody in that room has ever told her and nobody is going to.** **And then the ninth seat asks Ines Tarrant why she meant it, because she asked to be asked on 26 November and nobody was able to ask her, and she answers in about nine seconds, and it is the only reason any of the nine has ever given: if this turns into a job then the nearest person to the barrier is a woman with a job on a board, and in about four years somebody will use that to tell her what the barrier is for. She has not stopped and nothing followed from it and it did not go in the minute.** At about a quarter past eleven **a man who is not a member and is not on any list, who has followed all of it from a car park and could not tell what any of it was for, asks the only question anybody in this borough can ask him, because he is not on the agenda either:** *What is a service floor made of.* Nobody answers for nine seconds. **He says first that he is not going to answer it, and then answers, in eleven words, and it is a sentence and not a scheme and it is not the sentence anybody expected: A COUNTER. A PERSON WHO IS AT IT. A STATE ASKED FOR INSTEAD OF A LIST. A DATE. AND A BLANK LINE AT THE BOTTOM OF A BOARD THAT NOBODY IS REQUIRED TO WRITE ON.** And where it came from, and it is the sentence he has been carrying since 3 April without knowing it: that night two people said no — to a rota, to a board, to a form, to a clause, separately, in their own words, both of them right — and at six nineteen in the morning a gate went up anyway, and the reason it went up was that two people happened to be in a corridor and neither of them was required to be and neither of them was on anything; **and the only sentences that survive being written down are the small ones with a date on them.** And unasked: *a description is not an answer, and there are three people in this borough who know why and I am one of them.* Then the roll-call, because a body that cannot require anybody to do anything cannot be told what it will do, and both of those sentences are on a card on a wall in a Methodist hall and neither is going to be argued out of the other: the register at nineteen and the blank line blank; the September record and the August determination and the quarterly all public; **the second public record — fifty-one nodes, thirty-eight parties, thirteen shapes — unsigned, no name box, about four hundred other people's names on it, and about eleven people have taken the extract away and two have written back and been wrong and the clerk wrote *enclosure received, no comment* and that is in the file**; the reading rule in an inside pocket, used once, in about eleven seconds, and nobody is going to be told; **seven of nine, two chairs, and two chairs is a Tuesday**; the letter worked and the price of the letter is a sheet of A4 on a door and a woman who is a great deal more frightened and nobody was saved; **six words are his and two men are findable because he said them in rooms and he cannot unmake either**; **a woman's name in about eleven people and in no document, and two pieces of paper with it on them, and she said twice, out loud and before she did either, that she has not done it to be thanked**; **and the winter has one Sunday in it.** And at about twenty to twelve, with four people still talking in a corridor outside, **he says the thing he has been carrying since the twenty-third of April, out loud, once, in a room, in daylight, and it is the last door in this volume and it does not open all way: A BUILDING HAS AN ADDRESS. IT IS THE PEOPLE WHO HAVE NO LOCATION OF RECORD. AND A PANEL CANNOT WEIGH A CLAIM FROM A PERSON IT CANNOT LOCATE.** The room does not move.
